The Office's own terminal offers a **`Suggested correction`** — close line six of the return, the discrepancy resolves, no further action required — and **Joon refuses it on the page in his own hand: *a line is not a discrepancy if the thing it is reporting is true.*** Line six is **persons recovered from site**, added overnight in week four with a revision letter, a real box in a real form that nobody can use, because it attaches to an incident and **Kestrel 2042 is an equipment-failure designation and an equipment failure does not carry passengers.** Cliff Marner is six days out, fifty-six pounds, not angry with the hall, and precise about which form he is angry with; he has a service number that opens four doors in another city and a desk in that city with his name and a case number and no event. **Elias writes CLIFF MARNER on the ward wall in chalk beside Perpetua Oyelaran's, and Cliff says it is wrong, and it is, and then he explains the whole of what he has been asking for: that it be spelled the way he spells it, with two r's, because a booking system and a doctor in 2011 got it wrong and one of them was corrected by him in person at a desk.** He asks for it to be written down **before he is too tired to say it.** Then Rusk's covering letter arrives with the sentence the volume stands on: the absence of a passenger from the Kestrel response list **is being treated as an administrative error in the return.** Inez Sato's chalk goes underneath, unasked for: **AND THE HALL IS NOT ABOVE IT, AND WE ARE NOT GOING TO LET IT LOOK LIKE WE ARE.**

### Chapter 54 — The Inventory
Monday, day thirty-seven, from 07:58. Two clerks, a supervisor, a handcart and a receipt. The terminal goes first at 08:14 and takes the four-C line with it; two anchor lamps; the corridor repeater and the corridor light — **and Inez Sato puts one word on the table and everybody hears it: the dark hours stop being two and start being four**, which lands on a nineteen-year-old on a powered frame and on a bed at the clinic. Then the sealed node's access codes, the only thing taken that cannot be replaced in a week with paper and a person. **The eleven boxes in the garage go** in the back of an Office vehicle at 10:20, and **the bus copy is in neither this building nor an Office one**, and Joon moves the burden of proof onto the person carrying it and asks for a receipt number rather than a name, **and the name is not required.** The demand for the residents' file is made and **is a demand and not a seizure**, and the supervisor says in advance that there is no box on the form for a refusal either. **The last clerk runs out of forms at 14:20 and writes the impounded-items column in chalk on the ward wall, in a fourth column, because the wall is the only surface in the room with a column on it.** Nadi Bell reads all twenty-eight lines back in the order written. **Line fourteen is wrong** — a woman's oxygen cylinder as *items, 2* when it has Sena Ambrose's name on the tape in Lena Mir's hand — and the correction takes four minutes: the name in full, the number struck with one line, initialled, and Lena Mir's own initials beside it, **the only place in the cooperative's history where an Office clerk and a ward nurse stand in the same column in the same ink.** **Elias writes the receipt copy in his own hand, all twenty-eight lines, both sets of initials, and gets it right the first time**, and understands that the only thing he can still do with skill is be accurate about his own defeat.

### Chapter 55 — Della Marner
Tuesday, day thirty-eight, the midpoint, forty minutes on a bad line. **Della Marner filed on the sixth of September, day zero, and has been at a regional transit family-reception desk in Cordell for thirty-eight days.** She is the most prepared person Elias has spoken to in five weeks: she has a case number, a date, a paper from a transit authority, and she was told *kindly* by a woman with a queue that if her husband was on the Kestrel service he would be on the response list. **There is no response list, because there was no incident with people on it.** Mara stops Elias from saying *I know* and makes him say the arithmetic. What Della wants is not an apology — **apologies are for people who did something** — she wants his name on a piece of paper she can hold and she wants somebody to have to write it down. **And she says the sentence the volume is built on: a classification is not a mistake, so calling it a mistake means nobody has to find out who wrote it.** She has written down *that's not my department*, said kindly, by a woman at a desk. **Elias tells her everything, including the part that makes him look worst: he can prove to her at a table that her husband was inside that ground for thirty days and cannot prove it to her institution, and he will not soften that with a story about a machine, because the machine is not what got him out.** At the end she asks for the two words in his own notebook, and a young woman in a Cordell corridor says she will write them but asks whether the lady wants the surname spelled a particular way, and Della says **no, write it the way he said it.** Elias comes off the telephone and does not feel better and does not feel worse. **He feels faster**, notices it himself, and underlines one line in the notebook. Cliff Marner is asleep in a chair in the front room, alive, eight days out.

### Chapter 56 — The File Is Not the Office's
Wednesday, day thirty-nine, the front room, twenty-eight people. Item 11 is the file of the residents' body and **there is no line on the page for a resident to say no on.** Elias reads it four times, puts it on the table, says his two sentences, and walks to the end of the street and back twice for fifty minutes, and Petar Lisk writes that down and nobody has ever taken it off the wall. **Inez Sato names all three parts of the cost before she pays any of them** — the hall becomes *the obstruction* in a document with a case number on it, the power arrangement is now visible to somebody from the Office, and two households on Bell Row will hear about it before she gets to their doors — and she refuses to let the room use the Office's box, because she has watched a form writer pick *not applicable* when a person says no, which is the nearest box and the shortest and is a lie a stranger reads as a decision by a person who is never in the room. **The vote is nineteen no, four yes, five not voting.** Osei Kwarteng is one of the four, gives a reason about his wife's bed at a clinic, and asks for the reason to be written down. **Inez does not overrule him.** She writes the four under her refusal, same ink, same size, and says it does not go under and is not a footnote, and that **fair is not what she is able to do this week; careful is.** **Rusk comes to the ward on his own time on a Wednesday evening** and says the file is the residents', that Finding 3 is the Office's own practice, that he is bound by it, and that the Office will take a power summary and not the cards — and then says the most dangerous sentence in the batch: **then you have declined, and I am required to record that you declined, and it will be read by people who will not read the rest of it.** He is not warning a mood; he is telling the truth about a document, and the document is his. Inez makes the transcript name Osei Kwarteng, the date, and the reason about the clinic out loud. **It is the only page in the Kestrel inquiry of 2042 that names a resident's reason for something.** Nadi Bell comes outside and gives Elias the count in a movement-record sentence with a tense in it, and he writes it down, and the writing down is the whole of what he has.

### Chapter 57 — Five Questions
Thursday, day forty, the Office's inquiry room, 10:00 to 16:00, with a recorder, a microphone, a paper file, and a meal at half past two. **Nine chairs for nine speakers, Rusk at the far end of the table, Vane by the wall at the window.** **No signature line and no attestation, at the Deputy Director's written insistence, which Solano notes is the first time in the office's history.** **One:** eleven sentences, and *the corridor behaved* struck out on Elias's own request, because it is an opinion in the mouth of a witness. **Two:** Tomas's expired plan as fact and not as judgment, with the nine lines left as the judgment. **Three:** Mara's answers, the disclosure rule, *nobody inherits her*, and **the honest admission that the disclosure rule was written on the thirtieth and did not exist when the Chapter 46 question was asked** — offered before it can be found in October; Solano says *that is not in your favor*, Mara says *it is in the file*. **Four:** Joon's custody schedule, eleven items, *not in this building* four times, asked four times in four orders, and then **the sentence the rest of the volume has to survive, said by the woman who will write the findings: a private record is not an alteration of a public one. That is the answer, and the answer is correct.** Nobody in the room has a better sentence. **Five:** Inez's one pre-written sentence, and the discovery that there is no box for what a person said. Rusk then puts the whole cause on the table himself: a class-three credential that cannot be named, in a sector whose holder fields do not exist, opening a cabinet at 05:02 on a morning when two people were also in that cabinet — **and he says it is the only theory the office has, it is not a good one, and he needs it to hold or needs a better one before October.** The Spine is asked for the predecessor index and answers **`No answer`**, one line, and Joon copies it exactly as received with the time. Elias answers the fifth question with a sentence and not a name, and Rusk agrees with him in front of a recorder, **and that is the most frightening four minutes of the day.** Elias says his Friday sentence into the transcript and says out loud that it is the same sentence twice. **Nobody signed anything.**

### Chapter 58 — Nobody Has Read It
Friday, day forty-one. The demand is eleven pages and names every document in the team's custody individually, **and Tomas Vale's notebook of admissions is not on it.** Joon puts it face up at nine twenty: *it is not withheld, it is not to be produced subject to argument, it is not on a list made by a woman with a very good index.* **Tomas: it is free, and anything free is suspect, and I have been on the receiving end of free for eight years and it has cost me a career.** So he says it out loud with the two people he names: a cardboard box in a garage, taped in the side, gone in with the 2034 write-off, unread, unmoved, **eight pages**, written over four years at a kitchen table when he had made a decision he could not justify to a person or to a form. **He tells them because yesterday a Deputy Director demanded every document by name and his name is in the envelope and the document is not in it, and he would rather hand it over than be found to have kept it, and he would like nobody to write down that it was brave.** He asks whether it may be read in a room with Mara and Joon and no copy and no summary. **Mara says no. Joon says no, and wants the refusal on the page in the same ink as the asking, because in five years it would be a story about a team that was given a question and hurried to answer it. Tomas says no as well.** It stays unread. **Elias, in the doorway, says nothing at all and it takes him more effort than anything he does that day**, because he wanted to say *I will take care of it* and caught it four seconds in. **At 14:20 the Rescue Service suspends him from seam assignment** and gives him hose certification and inventory at a depot on the far side of the estuary, and he does not argue, because arguing has never once done anything. **The correction, made once:** Anja Ro says the man behind the wall is *a name somebody decided not to have*, and Joon says **no** — that is the one man who decided, and **a refusal is a record, and he did not have a name taken from him, he took it out** — and it is the only one either of them will ever get to keep. The chapter's last ordinary beat is a hose test: **Elias writes two hundred and ten because that number is in the front of his head, and it is two hundred and ten meters of migrating void under a street people walk over, and Mara reads over his shoulder and says it is one hundred and eighty-two**, and he draws one line through his own figure and writes hers, and says that in a year it will be a piece of evidence in a file about him.

### Chapter 59 — What the Word Is For
Saturday, day forty-two, 09:29 to after five, the second sitting. **Rusk reads the Office's case for two hours and eleven minutes and asks nothing.** It is not a monster and it is not stupid: the **2019 Salt Crossing** outfall, six dead inside eleven days, a finding that said *unlicensed* in every paragraph and *cause* in none; the **2029 Meridian** review, eleven dead behind a single approved outcome, and the one-instruction-one-route rule with a unilateral reroute power that came out of it. He says, to Elias's left and without looking at him, that the man sitting there sat on the same panel, and does not use it as a weapon. **The shape: an unlicensed, unrecorded, self-governed practice is a cause of these events, because the events recur wherever that practice is tolerated — not the only cause, a cause** — and it is offered for demolition in the room. **It survives an hour.** Elias gets half of it: **no designation would have permitted entry into that corridor on the twenty-ninth day**, and Rusk concedes that and gives back the other half, because a licensed entry would have gone past the 11.0 m boundary in the morning and been blamed for it. **That is not a trap. It is arithmetic.** Mara confirms a page of her own in the exhibit file and says **a document in an exhibit file is the only place in the world where a thing that worked is read by strangers**, and if a page of hers is ever used as an example of a process that failed she will be back in the room in a week. Joon produces the counter-case in order with the times on it: the blank field filled in accurately by somebody who was not there, the alignment offered and refused in writing, **and the receipt with a stranger's chalk on it.** **Rusk has already been to the wall**, on his own, on a Friday night, and stood in front of it for four minutes looking for an author, **and what came back was nothing** — and he reads the fourth column aloud, all twenty-eight lines, and says **that is not a gap in your evidence, that is its shape, and I have spent my morning reading a theory that says the shape is the danger.** And when Elias says *so the counter-case is not persuasive*, Rusk says **it is good, and it is not persuasive, and I am telling you both in one sentence so that you cannot have one without the other.** Then **the four words, read with their authors** — not used, withdrawn, declined, administrative error — **plus unresolved, which is not theirs, from a family-reception desk in Cordell on a case open since the sixth of September, in a clerk's hand, because she did not know** — and the finding: **not one of the five was written by a person hiding anything; every one is a system's honest word, produced by a form, a workflow, or a hurry, and every one of them takes a person out of a sentence and puts nothing in the space where the person was.** Rusk says the thing he cannot do: **I cannot tell you what a system gains by having fewer contradictions in it. My office is not built to hold that question.** He accepts every item and rules on none, **because an inquiry that rules on contested evidence becomes a trial and he is not putting a Deputy Director's stamp on a verdict.** Then he produces the **2039 consolidation schedule** — a store location, a volume, a box, a date, **a unit number in a block that is not a person's block, and a holding number** — and says the Office has had his Director's minute about Cordell for twenty-five days and has never had the schedule. **Nobody treats it as a revelation; Elias notices once, alone, that he has been carrying the same sentence since Chapter 33's Tuesday, and does not say it.** The payoff: **a holding number is the only thing in this business that crosses a regional boundary in a spoken sentence**, and without it his own letter comes back stamped *insufficient identifier* and gets filed. And he says what the letter is worth before Elias can find out. The last exchange: Elias can live behind *a private record is not an alteration of a public one*, and Rusk tells him why that is a problem and not a comfort — **you can live behind it because it is true, and if it were false it would cost you nothing. Being right is not the same as being safe.** And the last thing anybody says to Elias in this batch: **you have won the part I can give you, and I want you to understand that the part I can give you is small and I chose it.**

### Chapter 60 — A Box and No Name
Sunday, day forty-three, the batch close. Petar reads the board at seven, both halves of the old question, the case number, and the clerk's fourth column, and nobody answers the second half. **The day is already done by the time Elias comes out of the records room at half past ten** — the round on the nail, three hands on the four draws, the laundry running, Ansel on his frame, nobody looking up — and Lena Mir tells him to stop standing there until somebody says the round is right today. **Emil Baros asks for a function and asks to be asked first, and names it: keep the hall's copy of the fourth column, check it against the Office's when theirs turns up, and know the difference by heart.** Inez promises a vote after Tuesday and names both costs, **and the sheet still has his name on it and nothing after it.** The keeper's line is still not his and Lena does not explain herself twice. **Cliff Marner asks Tarin Voss whether they have got him**, and Tarin tells him: he searched for himself on the ward terminal two weeks ago, with Joon standing next to him, and got *patient, nineteen, male, capacity applied, target of record withdrawn at subject's request* with no letter of his name on it and a form at the bottom saying the withdrawal is a finding and not an absence. It is correct practice, it is the only reason the strongest page they hold is still valid, and in four years his name on it would make a sentence about a boy. **It does not need fixing, and there is no form anywhere in the city for taking a name out, and he is not going to be the person who writes one at nineteen and gets it wrong.** Cliff asks Elias at the door whether his name is going to be in it, and Elias says **I do not know**, and Cliff says that is the fourth true thing anybody has said to him since he came up on the thirtieth and that he is going to hold on to it. **The call at half past four:** the custodian is in a store room with a cart and will not come out for a telephone; a deputy reads the holding number back; **the box went on a conservation cart on Friday, is on the second cart, comes back Tuesday, and on Wednesday goes back on a shelf and becomes a shelf to look for.** Nobody asked for it. **The slip has a reference, a time, a destination, and no requester's name, because public records requests are anonymous by design.** Joon says both readings out loud and refuses to choose, and wants the refusing on a record, and Elias — holding the schedule — **names the resemblance once, in one sentence, and stops: Director Arden put a letter into that building on the twenty-third of September and said it would be read on the Wednesday by somebody with a queue, and nobody in this room has ever been told what came back; a request with a box number on it is not a letter, and a queue is a queue.** Joon writes both in one ink and chooses neither. **The visit is booked for Tuesday day forty-five.** The depot gives a hose slot back in writing with a number on it, and it is the only institution in five weeks that has given anything back without a hearing. **In a corridor Elias says the word out loud — faster — and Mara gives it back unsoftened: that is the feeling you had on Tuesday of the fourth week, and you know what it is, and so do I. Say it back. Neither of us.** At seven, Petar writes two more lines, the second **underneath** the old question and not replacing it: **THEY CAN TAKE THE BOXES. THEY CANNOT TAKE THE WALL. I READ IT EVERY MORNING** — and, smaller, **WHO BENEFITS WHEN A BRANCH IS OFFICIALLY DELETED?** **The chapter puts no count on the wall.** Both questions are read every morning from here to the end of the volume.

---

# Volume 02 Chapter Summaries — Batch 0002 (Chapters 61–70)

**Day zero is Saturday the sixth of September 2042.** All dates below are derived from that and every weekday was checked against `outline/volume-02.md`. This batch is **Mon 44 (61) · Tue 45 (62) · Wed 46 (63) · Thu 47 (64) · Fri 48 (65) · Sat 49 (66) · Sun 50 (67) · Mon 51 (68) · Tue 52 (69) · Wed 53 (70).** **Tuesday day forty-five is the Cordell visit and the team is out of Havenport from the Monday night before it until the Monday night of day fifty-one.**

### Chapter 61 — Four to Six Weeks
Monday, day forty-four, the ward, all day. Petar reads the board at seven and the second half of the question is still a bracket with nothing under it. **Della Marner's four-to-six-week estimate was quoted on day fourteen, so the near end expired on Saturday day forty-two and nothing came**, and the hall learns that through nineteen minutes of a queue on its one telephone line, and **the batch's first lesson is that what the impoundment took was not equipment, it was a way of asking.** Ansel is asleep on his frame at half past nine and will be awake at nine tonight and again at three, because the dark hours are four now. **Cliff Marner asks Elias whether he is going with them, and Elias cannot answer, and the hall cannot answer it, and only Mara can, and Mara cannot until a physician has been in the room.** **Elias tells twenty-three people in the front room that he is not the person who decides this — the first time in five weeks he has said anything like it, and the reason is his own suspension and not his character** — and Inez Sato takes the meeting: two people go, not four, and she names all three parts of the cost first, the tin leaving the building for six days included. **Lena Mir states the cost of Mara staying: no physician in the building since the first week, and two of those days are a fact about a nineteen-year-old on a powered frame and a bed at the clinic.** **Mara applies her own disclosure rule out loud, to her own decision, in the order the rule requires, and says nobody is overruling her because there is nothing to overrule.** **The fourth column is copied by four hands from nine at night until one, into a biscuit tin, with a rubber band round it twice, and Petar Lisk reads all twenty-eight lines back and stops at a hole and says he is not going to fill it and is not going to say where it is.** The car is booked for a quarter past seven on Tuesday and costs forty-one dollars. **Elias writes something down at half past one in the morning, and understands that nobody asked him for a decision all day, and that being trusted with one is not the same as being asked for one, and the second thing is harder.**

### Chapter 62 — Forty Minutes by an Ordinary Road
Tuesday, day forty-five, out of the city and in another one, morning to evening. **Forty minutes on an ordinary road, a tin on Elias's knees, and Joon Park driving because the booking needed a name and the name was the hall's records keeper and the hall's records keeper was not him.** **The records center and the regional transit family-reception hall are the same building, same material, same decade, ninety meters apart, with different management, different budgets and no relationship whatever except a corridor, and the young woman who wrote Cliff Marner's name on a pad on the ninth day had been standing that far from a store room with nine credentials in it for forty-five days. Two budgets, not a conspiracy.** **Rufus Aitken, 51, nineteen years on the counter, asks first whether they are the person the holding number was given to, tells them they are bearers, and says out loud in front of Joon that bearers get the same eleven days as everybody else so nobody can tell them afterward it was special.** The box is on a conservation cart and comes back at twenty to four, and after that it is a shelf and not a box. **He will not be told what the box is for, and he has read a page out loud in that interview room exactly once in nineteen years and does not do it for them, and says why: a man came in about his wife, and a page is not an act of care, it only feels like one, and he stopped doing it on purpose.** **Elias reads the room's rules off the walls, which is what he is actually for, and asks the question nobody wanted answered — what happens to a request on a day the building is shut — and learns the re-dating rule, and learns that the store room has a Sunday public hour from ten to twelve that a preservation levy pays for and nobody has ever used, and gets the Wednesday slot nobody asked for.** **Around one o'clock he understands the number: it works, and it works for anybody who has it, and Rusk had handed them a sentence anybody who can read a date can say out loud, and he had been describing the building and not them.** **Wendell Pryce says in a corridor, to a young man with a trolley who has not asked him anything, that he worked the west apron line and never held that credential, and will say it to a fourth and a fifth, and is not doing it for the team; and then closes a door for them, out loud, to their faces: the credential was issued in blocks, signal huts knew the block, and there is not one person in that city who could name the man.** The second cart comes back at twenty to four. **Elias says *I know what happens this morning* in the car park at ten past five, alone, eleven days after Friday the thirty-fourth, and writes down four things two strangers told him, and does not know what happens on Thursday either and notices that he has not said so.**

### Chapter 63 — The Form With a Box For It
Wednesday, day forty-six, the records center, and **the box goes back on a shelf today, and after today it is a shelf and not a box and you are looking for a shelf.** Shelf 14C, a steel unit, a printed label and no index card, and Aitken reads the holding number off it twice. The 4C renumbering file is eleven centimeters thick out of a gray box onto a gray table under a bulb. **The form is regional, not Office, and Joon Park says it out loud: nothing in the Office's practice reaches it, whatever happened on it happened without a correction, a withdrawal, a finding or a copy, and nobody could have been disciplined and nobody was protected.** **The form has eleven printed fields. Nine are about the sector, block, sequence, date, sheet count and store. The tenth is a ruled box with HOLDER over it and it is empty, on every sheet in the file.** **The eleventh field is printed and says `IF A FIELD IS NOT CARRIED FORWARD — STATE THE REASON`, and it has two words in it in a small neat hand: `not applicable`.** Aitken, from a doorway: the box asks what happened to the *field*, the design of the form is deliberate and good, and he would fight anybody who called it a cover. **Joon refuses a meaning out loud in a building that is not his, and it costs him, because in a hall a legible refusal is free and here it is a thing he does to a woman who did nothing wrong, and he asks for that to be written down.** **Elias does not ask a second question**, though he wanted to, using the voice that gets him what he wants. They file a real request at four minutes past twelve and are quoted **four to six weeks from the twenty-second of October: the nineteenth of November at the near end, the third of December at the far end.** The center's clerk points out that a holding number is not a request and does not start a clock. **In the lobby Elias says the thing he has been not saying: it is not a box for hiding a person, it is a box for not carrying one, and that is worse, and it is correct, and not one thing anybody in that building could have done differently.** At ten past one he telephones Ash Street, and Inez Sato gives him the vote — **twelve for the function, eleven against, five not voting** — and tells him to ring Emil Baros, and not to tell him it was close because he will hear it in four minutes. **Joon tells Elias to ask him about a ramp for four minutes first, and it works.** Baros says at the end: *Nine days I asked for it and nobody asked me a question. Twelve people voted for it this morning and not one of them asked me whether I wanted it.* And the block has a person in the center's own current index, because the block is still in use. **Her name is Rosalind Teague and she is on the second floor of a building he is standing in.**

### Chapter 64 — The Family-Reception Desk
Thursday, day forty-seven. **The decision is taken in a corridor ninety meters away with a physician in the room and Elias on the telephone, and it is not taken for the plot.** Mara puts her back to a wall on his side of the line. **Dr. Imogen Sarkar has been looking at Cliff Marner since the thirtieth, she is in the room, and she has said that if she has to be talked out of it she will not argue with Mara, and Mara has not tried.** The reason is given out loud: **seventeen days**, four hours in a train in the third week with a change at Ash Junction and a coach of strangers, and forty minutes on an ordinary road in a car is not that journey, and it is not the same question. **Mara refuses to write the number in a document and says she will take it out of his hand if he does.** Cliff says yes when asked whether he wants to go, knowing it is not the end of it. **Four hours in the building, a bed at the Cordell hospital at eight, and Elias is to be the man who says it — and Mara tells him he will not have a say, he will have a job, and not to confuse them.** **Della Marner is in the same chair by the same window, forty-seven days in, and does not stand up.** **Yvette Roche, twenty-four, the clerk whose voice came down a line on the eighth day, says the one true thing in that building and says it once: the roster has `Clifford Aldis Marner`, station attendant, Cordell terminus, rostered for the early turn on the sixth of September, clocked on at four minutes to six, and there is no incident recorded against that service on that date because the service did not stop, and four hundred people went to work on it — and she has been writing `unresolved` in a box since the sixth of September.** **The paper is a family-reception case extract: case number, date opened, the name as the family gave it, and the status `unresolved`, signed by a clerk and sent to a house and not an office, because an extract sent to an office is a document about a case.** Della Marner puts it in the plastic wallet and does not say thank you and says *Right*, and then asks whether he is all right. **Cliff's four doors are named** — the end-of-line gate and platform, the stationmaster's office, the relay hut annexe, the duty store and mess — **and the batch says what a service number is and is not, and that what got him out was a woman in a hall writing his name down twice and a nineteen-year-old on a frame and a hall counting him in a number that was not a count.** **Cliff and Yvette Roche are two people who had never met each other and neither of them asked for it, and he says *you are the one who wrote it* and she says she has never once known what she was being brave about.** He sits with his wife for fifty minutes with the wallet open on her knee and Elias does not listen, because listening to a thing that is not yours is a form of taking. **At half past seven Elias says it, and Cliff says *I know*, and then: *You are the fifth one. The first four had their reasons. You have got hers.*** And Elias is relieved, and it does not help, and he notices that too, and writes it down in the car with the date on the page.

### Chapter 65 — Two Words *(midpoint)*
Friday, day forty-eight, the records center, second floor, eleven desks. **Rosalind Teague, thirty-nine, twelve years, opened the file, and she says first that she spent a second and a half being pleased that somebody had asked and a second and a half being frightened, and that both are hers and neither is Elias's.** **The two words are `not applicable`, in the correct box, on the correct day, in the correct space, and they are the correct answer to the question the form actually asks, and the form's question is not the question anybody wanted answered.** She was the lowest person at the table for eleven weeks and did the part nobody wants. **Her sentence: *I answered the question on the form. It was not the question I would have asked.*** She was told by a man whose name she will not give that the fields were not going to be carried, that it was a consolidation, and that the names were on a succession list in the regional office, and she believed him, and **she did not ask, and not because she was told not to.** She has been back twice in nine days and there is nothing to go back to. **She would change the words tomorrow if the box had asked her who, and she refuses to be described as brave in a corridor, because she was competent at a form and there is a difference and she has got quite good at it.** Joon asks the two questions that matter and gets two no's, and when she asks for the difference between *no* and *no, none that I have ever seen*, he gives it to her and writes that she asked for it. **He records her account in full, in her words, with the date, reads the page back, and adds nothing.** **When she asks what the site produced, Elias refuses a number *and* a name, and says the whole of why in one go: there is a person under a street in his city, her name is on a wall in chalk, the only sentence ever true about her is a sentence with a proper name in it and a tense written in advance by a woman who was alive and was asked, and he will fill in nobody's space.** She writes *no figure was given* and says she did not push. **The team does not put her name in, and Elias says the price out loud in the corridor afterwards: the document is weaker without her name, and he will not be able to put the other half back.** **Wendell Pryce tells him he did it well and that he does not know how anybody does it twice, and asks for the date, and goes up the stairs.** Joon's hand on his shoulder for a second and a half on the way to the stairs, and nothing said, and the date, and the tin in the boot.

### Chapter 66 — Nine Is Nine
Saturday, day forty-nine, the center's half day, nine till one. **Wren Okonjo can give a credential count in about four seconds and cannot tell you anything else at all, because a count has four things on it and one of them is a number, and it has never had a holder field and is not going to get one.** Sector 4 C, that sequence, that year: **nine.** The sequence list is a different document in a different drawer and nobody in that building would sign for nine numbers with nothing next to them on a Saturday. **Aitken brings a certified count, signed and stamped, with the certificate stating that the figure is the figure in the regional sequence register on the date of issue, and he explains where it came from: a Deputy Director's letter with a case reference and a public booking under a holding number arrived within four days of each other, the center told the other institution in writing, the answer said the booking is not connected, Aitken says that is a true answer and also an answer nobody had to check anything to give, and a Counsel arrived at the door this morning.** **Elias proposes the one shortcut available — nine lines, their names, their document — and Joon refuses it, and it is about a rule and not a principle: a document we make is a document we own, and we would be the first people in the whole of this to have an opinion about those nine, and we would be putting it on a page in a column with a heading on it.** **Counsel Renata Solano, forty-seven, came on purpose and says why in one sentence: in October she has to be able to say what she did and did not do in a regional records center in the week of the twenty-first of October.** Three questions: has the archive been asked to hold anything (no, and she wants it on a form); does the residents' body hold a document it cannot produce on demand (**yes, and no, and she says the second is also the correct answer and it is not kindness, she would rather they refused in front of her than vanish and be talked about later**); is there a person in the building who has not answered a question from this office (Joon says no, and Elias says nothing at all). **Wendell Pryce is in the lobby and tells them to ask for the store log of the 2035 pull and not for the sheets, and says why: the sheets are what you want and the log is what is in the building, and he would rather somebody who needs it had it than him, and he has checked that it was written down in May.** Elias is tired in a way he has not been tired, and understands that he has spent six weeks being angry at a system for being correct, and came here to prove that a system that was not correct existed, and found a number, **and that the correct behavior of a well-run archive is the only thing anybody wanted all week and is the one thing nobody in Havenport will be able to explain to a Deputy Director.**

### Chapter 67 — The Store of Older Sheets
Sunday, day fifty, and the center is shut except for the store room's public hour from ten to twelve, which exists because a preservation levy pays for it. **Bettina Hoyle has done it for six years and four people have come and two of them were lost and she is not sorry about the two.** Aitken changes a bulb himself on a step stool and does not accept help. **The 4C sheets are not the renumbering sheets: they are sheets from a store of older sheets, pulled in the spring of 2035 for the regional mapping consolidation, and the store's movement book records the pull on a line dated THE FOURTH OF MARCH 1998 — thirty-seven years before the renumbering it records — signed by a unit in a block and not by a person.** **Aitken's honest institutional answer, which he labels incomplete himself and then goes and stands at the end of the aisle for: a store is re-purposed, a shelf is renumbered, and the book recording which shelf is which is not renumbered in the same week, because the two people have two jobs and one of them is in a hurry on a Tuesday.** **Bettina Hoyle says the other half and says it first: a book is a thing a person can be argued with, and that is the whole difference between a book and a form, and she would like it to be somebody.** **Joon Park sees the shape — the aisle log, a book somebody can argue with, a field that is either there or is not, a form and a hurry — and refuses it out loud in a store room in another city, and asks for the refusal to go into the store's own day book because that is what a book is for, and it does, and it takes forty seconds.** **Elias opens his mouth to say *this is exactly what happened in aisle nine* and does not say it, and does not understand why until the corridor of the transit hall at five past one: in a hall a refusal is free because the hall is his; in a store room it costs thirty seconds and a day-book entry and nothing else, and the day it costs nothing is the day it stops being a refusal and becomes a habit.** **He wants to be told, and does not push, and the not-pushing is the growth the batch actually pays for.** **They ask what may be photographed, and the answer is the movement book freely, with the archivist initialling every frame, one of the two visitors in the room at all times, and no original working sheet at all; a facsimile release is refused, and the reason is that a photograph is obviously a picture and a facsimile is not obviously anything, and that is what it is for.** They photograph the book for forty minutes and get four frames, and Elias writes **NOT YET. AND NOT BY US.**

### Chapter 68 — Monday, and the Nineteen Years
Monday, day fifty-one, the last morning in Cordell and the road back. **Constance Ilori, fifty-seven, copy supervisor, nineteen years, reads the whole of what they want in four minutes and names the three things that stand in the way: the movement book does not leave this building in any form; the 2035 form may not be photographed because it is a living form, and what may leave is a certified copy; and a copy fee of thirty-one dollars, which is a schedule, and there is no hardship rate, and she looked without being asked.** **The team's own hand transcription, made with a pencil on a sheet of the hall's own paper, is a derivative copy: it is registered in Cordell, compared against the original, and does not travel. Joon Park signs the lodge entry and will not let Elias sign it, and the price is said out loud: the only copy of that wording in anybody's handwriting is now in three places and none of them is a building you can walk into tonight.** **Joon will not ask a stranger to spend her own money and says so in the corridor. Elias asks once — the first time in five weeks he has asked anybody for anything, and it is a question and not a promise and not a plan — and is told no, and is then given something better: she cannot waive the fee and has never certified a copy under a store attendance in nineteen years, and she does it at two dollars forty instead of three dollars ten, and asks for two things, do not thank me and send the copy number to the day book on the shelf so one entry in that card index was made by somebody who was asked.** **She refuses the facsimile of the 1998 page and gives the reason, and Elias takes the no.** They leave with less than they came for and know exactly what they left. **At ten past four, from a payphone in a station waiting room, Mara answers out of the corridor with the records-room door shut, and Tomas Vale answers the Office form that has been on his desk since the day his name went in an envelope: *Yes. I will be available to give evidence at any future inquiry. I will make myself available, I will come, and I will not send anybody.* He wants it recorded in his words and not the form's, because the form asks about his intentions and his intentions are not the point. **Mara says the one thing and puts the handset down: *You believe the truth is better for him now. That is the most dangerous belief a man can hold in an inquiry, and there is no form anywhere in this business with a box for it.*** Joon reads the entry back twice, the second time with the time on it, and **neither of them says the word notebook on a telephone in a station.** They get in at a quarter past seven and Lena Mir is on the ramp with a lamp and somebody has put a new bulb in the corridor fitting and nobody knows who. Elias puts the tin on the table, takes the rubber band off, and then puts it back on, because Ru Sabar was right about a thing tied twice.

### Chapter 69 — What They Bring Back
Tuesday, day fifty-two. **Four sheets go on the Ash Street wall in nineteen hands in forty minutes and nobody is allowed to help, which was Petar's rule and Inez's backing**: the certified count of nine, the printed headings and the exact wording over the second box, the two words with the date and the store, and four lines describing the movement-book line with page and frame numbers and **no explanation**. **Not the residents' file, because the file can be demanded and there is no line on it for a resident to refuse on, and not a file, because the wall is a wall and nobody owns it and a Deputy Director could not find an author for it in four minutes.** **Nadi Bell states the other half and it is written underneath: being safe from being taken and being able to be asked are two different things, and today the hall is choosing the first one on purpose, and she is the person who wrote a movement record in a past tense precisely so there would be two versions, and she is not going to pretend it is not a cost.** **Emil Baros's line is on the sheet and he was not in the room, and he had insisted on being told eleven minutes ahead of a vote nobody had told him about, and he made Inez Sato write *the line says what it is and not what I wish it was*, and Lena Mir voted no, did not say why, and was not asked twice.** **Rusk telephones the hall, asks for Joon and then for Elias, and asks one question: *Did you find a name.* Elias says no. Rusk explains that the honest answer costs the team more, because a name would have been a sentence and a decent sentence and a document about a person produced by a team with a holding number that nobody in the inquiry could ever check, and that a search that produces nothing is the cheapest thing an institution ever does and he is the institution. He says he has written a report on a Monday and has not written it well and tells two men in a ward that. He asks for both refusals to be minuted, notes that the second is ten days after the first, asks to speak to Ms. Sato, says he is sorry about the wording of a letter and not sorry about the position, and is told the correct answer twice.** **The batch's answer is said out loud: nobody lost nine people in 2035, the renumbering was built so that losing them was a correct thing to do, and the printed box is the reason nobody had to decide anything.** And **the second half of the Chapter 50 question is still unanswered, and Elias says he still cannot say the word, and neither can Rusk, and asks for it to be minuted.** The material goes where the Office cannot serve on it, including **a copy of the copy in the county library's local-history room in Cordell with a catalogue card naming the residents' body and the people who wrote the papers.**

### Chapter 70 — The Date on the Page
Wednesday, day fifty-three, evening, Ash Street, the batch close. **The hall ran for six days without him and it had not once stopped, and the proof is that four people were doing a job when he came up the ramp and two of them did not look up.** Four carded draws signed in three hands, the laundry running, **Ansel awake at five in the evening, which means he was awake at three, and nobody was going to say so.** **Lena Mir's decision is waiting for him and it is hers and nobody asked for it: she asks for the four dark hours to go into the residents' file in her own hand with a date and her name and the reason, and Inez Sato refuses for two reasons, the second of which is Lena's own — the file is the thing under a demand, and there is one medical authority in a building of twenty-eight people and a name in a demanded file teaches everybody else which page to be frightened of — and Lena says she said it out loud so that it would be written down that she asked, and says she does not need the file because a nine-year-old wrote it on a wall.** **The four frames are in the records room with the year in pencil under the year in ink, and nobody in that building can say 1998 out loud, and no rule was made, and the reason is that a hall that says out loud it is looking for something has started a search, and a search gets a name, and a name gets a date on it, and a date gets somebody's why.** **Joon Park says *I am not writing what anybody thinks it means* for the fourth time, in a building with nobody from the Office in it, and it cost him nothing for the first time, and he noticed and has not decided what to do with having noticed.** **Petar Lisk reads the board at seven, reads the two questions and the four new sheets, and writes the closing line, which contains no number about people and answers neither question, and reads it twice to twenty-eight people: A BOOK IN ANOTHER CITY HAS A DATE ON IT THAT WE ARE NOT GOING TO SAY OUT LOUD IN HERE. FOUR FRAMES OF IT ARE IN THE RECORDS ROOM AND A SEAL ON A COUNT IS UNDER THE CLERK'S FOURTH COLUMN. PETAR L. — I AM NINE AND I HAVE READ IT TWICE.** **Elias came back with less than he took out, the ward is standing, and he is not the reason it is standing, and that sentence was false every day for six weeks and is true on Wednesday evening, and he let it be true, which cost him nothing.** The chapter closes on the seal and the tunnel standing, the eleven meters a length with a day on it, a man behind a wall fifty-three days in and unwritten at his own request, a signalman still paying ninety dollars a night, a woman of thirty-nine somewhere in another city finishing a shift who wrote two words correctly with no idea it was going to be the whole of what anybody had, **and the answer in five places: in a form, on a wall, on a library shelf in a county, and in a notebook in a garage, with a copy in a tin and a rubber band round it twice.**

# Volume 02 Chapter Summaries — Batch 0003 (Chapters 71–80)

**Day zero is Saturday the sixth of September 2042.** This batch is **Thu 54 (71) · Fri 55 (72) · Sat 56 (73) · Sun 57 (74) · Mon 58 (75) · Tue 59 (76) · Wed 60 (77) · Thu 61 (78) · Fri 62 (79) · Sat 63 (80).** **Chapter 75 is the volume midpoint and it is day fifty-eight, Monday the third of November. Day 75 is Thursday the twentieth of November and is not a midpoint of anything.** Batch word count: **23,318**, re-measured after the 2026-09-26 review-repair pass; it was 23,248 as first written.

### Chapter 71 — The Ordinary Week
Thursday, day fifty-four. **Elias Rook writes two hundred and ten on a hose certification form and the form means two hundred and ten and the foreman does not say one word about it**, because a test pressure is in pounds and a hose either holds or does not, and the whole of it took forty minutes, and he has done harder things in a wet stairwell in the second week of September and been better at them. He is back on Thursday for the other half of the job, which is inventory: a lamp count, a battery count and a form. **The hall has a pencil on a string and the pencil is the office's and the string is Inez Sato's**, and if it goes she wants it written down in the room that it went and not who took it. Four carded draws, three in an unfamiliar hand, and Ru Sabar's with her time on it. **Mara Okafor reads the form over his shoulder for four seconds and says there is nothing wrong with that page**, and puts a tick in the margin against his signature, and says the tick means the hose passed and does not mean the sample sheet agrees with the form, and it is the only notation in nine weeks that has meant exactly one thing. **He asks for both hose slots back in writing on the depot's own form with the dates on it**, and the foreman's letter is four lines: both slots returned, 06:30, **these will not be held twice**, and a fifth line that says he is the only man in that building who asked in writing for both at once and not to wait, **and that he will put it in no file because there is no file**. Elias reads the fifth line four times, tells nobody, and puts it in the notebook with the envelope. **He copies the four sheets out longhand on Wednesday and Thursday evening for the tin, and it is the hardest transcription he has ever done by hand, and there is nothing new in it.** Two evenings, nineteen lines in nineteen hands, and he understands at eleven o'clock on Thursday that the wall nobody can be given an order to produce and the copy on a library shelf in a county the Office has no building in are now **both the only copies**, and neither can be produced, and therefore neither can be corrected. **A record that cannot be taken is a record that cannot be corrected.** Constance Ilori's copy certificate arrives by post, one card, in an envelope addressed to *store attendance, shelf 3*, and he telephones the county library's local-history room and gives them the number and asks for it written down that he gave it to them, and is told the catalogue entry is **four hundred and thirteen** and that it is the only one on that card this year anybody rang about. **He keeps the envelope, and nobody asks him why, and the reason is that it is the first post in nine weeks addressed to a description of a room rather than a person, and a thing that can be delivered to a room can be produced by anybody who can find the room.** He does not sleep much, because a life with nothing in it turns out to be a different life and not a better one, and he cannot tell which of the two he would choose on a Tuesday morning. He stands in the dark corridor at ten past one because the hall is dark from midnight to four and the corridor light belongs to a woman who switches it on herself at seven and has never once been asked to switch it on earlier. **In the morning he asks Inez Sato, in the front room with four people in it, in the words the rule requires: can this building send anybody to Cordell again, and if it can, whose card goes back for a week?** She puts her pen down, which she does not do, and says ask me that properly on Friday at seven in the morning and not seven in the evening, and she will tell him why on Friday and not now.

### Chapter 72 — What a Second Trip Costs
Friday, day fifty-five, the depot on the far side of the estuary. **He telephones at ten past six to say he is not coming at half past six, and R. Dunleavy makes him say it while he is on the line and writes *not on site 0610, called, meeting on site* — not *unwell*, not *personal*, because those two words would be a thing he had to explain to a man in an office in March — and says *you are four hours late before you have started and I would rather know it at six ten than at eleven*.** The meeting is at seven in the morning with four people in it, and Inez Sato says why in one sentence: **Ru Sabar's card goes back at ten past six, and by seven in the evening half the people who have to be in this are in a clinic corridor or on a powered frame or asleep, and she would rather have a small meeting at seven than a large one in the evening, and would rather say that out loud than have four people go home and say the hall only meets when it is convenient.** Ansel Duarte comes in on his own, which takes eleven minutes, with a blanket and a book he is not reading, and Lena Mir had said he would be awake at four and he had been and nobody said so. Elias puts the depot form on the table face up, as instructed. **A certified copy is two dollars forty. A day in a car is forty-one dollars and has not gone up. The hall has nothing, because the last eleven hundred dollars went on a dry end in the second week. The hall's money is not a budget, it is a consent card** — every watt comes on somebody's card with their name on it, and the moment a thing costs money that is not somebody's power it comes out of somebody's power, and **the amount is not forty-one dollars, the amount is a week.** It will not be her card, because **it is not mine after six is a condition and not a preference**, and she has been asked twice this month to break somebody's condition for a document and the answer is the same both times. She says the number out loud so that anybody whose card is the week can stop it, and nobody stops it, because all four of them have given a card for something this month and not one will be the one who says *mine*. **Ansel Duarte asks whether the last trip was worth it** — not because he wants a fight but because he cannot walk to a hospital and the answer changes his week. **Elias cannot answer it and says so**, and Ansel says *that is the first honest thing anybody has said in this room in a fortnight, and it is not an answer*, and Inez Sato writes **no answer given.** **The hall does not spend a dollar on a second trip**, because the hall's money is a card, so the hall asks: the county library's local-history room will host a person from Havenport for the cost of a bus fare, the person is named in a book in that room the way a donor is named, and **the room may refuse to say who sent them, in writing, from her and not from the hall.** The room said yes in one sentence and then said the person will be somebody who can be asked what they are doing there. Joon Park, standing in the corner where he can see both doors, points out the public hour is Sunday ten to twelve and that whatever gets looked at gets looked at in two hours on a Sunday by somebody who can be asked. **Inez offers the trip to Elias, in front of the four of them, in the form the rule requires, and he says no, and gives two reasons, and the second one is the chapter: *I have spent nine weeks being the man everybody decides is the right one, and I have enjoyed it, and I did not know until this morning that I was enjoying it, and a man who enjoys being the right one is a man who cannot say no, and there is going to be a day when that costs somebody a card.*** Inez records the refusal as his to make and not hers to overrule. **Ansel points out that somebody ought to be told what the depot form says, because in six weeks there is going to be somebody in a room who says the man from Ash Street did not have a job in October, and there is going to be a piece of paper in front of them that says he had one and it was on a Tuesday and he was on it.** He gets to the far bank at half past ten, works the last row with the foreman for eighty minutes, and the foreman writes the time he arrived and also writes *unfinished* ruled out, because a man four hours late and unfinished is a different fault from a man four hours late. **Next Friday's slot is gone. These will not be held twice.** He says the kindest thing anyone has said to him in nine weeks and then says he put it in no file, because there is no file, and will deny it if repeated. Outside, the fog is coming off the estuary and there is a bus to Cordell four times a day at two dollars sixty each way, and he knows both figures because he looked them up on Thursday night and has not told anybody.

### Chapter 73 — The Shelf Nobody Had a Reason To Look At
Saturday, day fifty-six, the records room on Ash Street. **Joon Park builds a request out of four things — a subject, a shelf range, a span, and a certified copy at the end if one exists — and will not sit down, and it takes four hours.** The rule: **you cannot ask for a book by describing it, and a request shaped around what you already know is not a request, it is a conclusion with a form on it.** The subject line reads *regional signal maintenance series, Civic Spine relay, Meridian — paper working volumes, 1990–2005.* **There is no blade in it, no field, no signature and no year, and Joon writes down that the request is honest before anybody can say afterwards that he narrowed it later.** A span of fifteen years asks a stranger to walk a trolley for a stranger's handwriting, but **a shelf range is a thing a clerk can bring on a trolley without anybody saying a year.** Nobody in that building has said a year out loud in nine days and nobody made a rule about it, and the rule was made in the last week by twenty-eight people who never discussed it. **The second problem is the man in the building.** Aitken will not be told what a box is for and is more difficult than he was on Wednesday: **the last thing he carried out of that building for a member of the public with no form was nineteen years ago, a man with a question about his wife, one page, and he read it out and thought *heard* and *told* were two different men, and he was right, and it changed nothing, and the file did not change, and he has never found out whether the man read it.** He will not carry things for people who have not asked in a form. He says the request is on the counter and the eleven days start on Monday, and he says the eleven days are the same for a holding number and a piece of paper, **and he says it again in the same voice at the same temperature when Elias asks him to say it where Joon can hear.** What comes back on the Saturday is a clipboard with a shelf range and a line in Aitken's hand: *run, 5 vols, paper, 1996–2001, Civic Spine relay maintenance, Meridian, no index card.* Then Aitken does something unexpected and goes and gets the whole store's shelf list and puts his thumb on one line. **Five volumes, paper, bound, working logs, a relay in the west district, maintained by somebody in a unit and not by a person, from ninety-six to two thousand and one, in the building since the twenty-thirty-nine consolidation put a shelf of Civic Spine papers next to them; nobody has ever asked for them, there is no card on the run, and Aitken had been in that store eleven years and did not know they were on that shelf until this morning.** And then the only thing he has got: **in that building the answer to somebody not asking a thing and the answer to somebody asking it are the same document, and if nobody ever asked, then in nineteen years somebody will go through the file and find that nobody asked, and that is a worse thing to have in a building than a line in a day book** — and the day book is the store's, and it is the only book there that anybody outside his building has ever been asked to write in, and that was Joon, ten days ago, and it is still there. The third lever is the store room's Sunday public hour, ten to twelve, which exists because a preservation levy pays for it, and the levy comes out of a county budget Bettina Hoyle's desk sits inside, **and a preservation levy buys a public hour and does not buy anybody's goodwill.** **Joon takes the Saturday bus and files the request at the counter between nine and one and rides back on the four o'clock, two dollars sixty each way with the hall agreeing to one, so he is out one fare and did not ask anybody for it.** He comes back with a page torn off a day book with a date and two sets of initials on it and nothing else, and says the Sunday hour is not his to spend and that if it is used then the man who uses it has to be somebody a woman in that building can ask a question of. **Elias says no to the second trip again, and the second no is easier, and he notices that it is easier and writes nothing about it.** He is left in a building running without him for a second time and finds he can do the work — the lamp count, the four draws, the corridor walk at ten past one because it is his turn on the sheet and the sheet has no clause about a nine-year-old. At about two he understands there is nothing in the building that needs him, and that the reason is not that it has failed, and that he had been looking at the front door every twenty minutes since ten, and that he was glad, and that the gladness was the exact shape of the thing he had said out loud on Friday about enjoying being the right one. He writes the date under it. **What is on the shelf in Cordell is five bound volumes of paper, standing there since the cart of twenty thirty-nine, and nobody has ever asked for them, and there is nothing on any form anywhere that says somebody should have.**

### Chapter 74 — A Book Is A Thing A Person Can Be Argued With
Sunday, day fifty-seven. The bus at ten past eight, forty minutes, two dollars sixty, and Elias Rook is the only person on it going to a records center. The key is on a hook and there has been one on that hook every Sunday for six years and the woman under it is **Bettina Hoyle**, and she tells him before he is through the door that he is the third visitor of the year and that the first two were lost, and that she is not sorry about the two. **And then she refuses him, in advance, in the form of a refusal that is not one: she is not going to hand a run of volumes to a man from a town that lost a tunnel, and lets it be a story about a man from a town that lost a tunnel, in her building, on a Sunday, out of her key.** He came with three sentences prepared and loses all three. She wants to know what he is before she decides what she is saying. **Joon Park's rule bites and he says it on the Saturday step of the hall, once, before Elias goes: *the thing you are about to do is the thing you did with the nine lines. A request that is shaped around what you already know is not a request. It is a conclusion with a form on it. You are the man I said that to seven days ago in a room in that city about nine people.*** The volumes come out on a trolley at ten o'clock, five of them, and Bettina Hoyle pushes it and it takes a person on each end, because a bound paper volume on castors is not a book you carry, it is an object you move. **Sixteen millimeters of board. Five volumes, ninety-six to two thousand and one. A working log, not a report. A working log is a book a person wrote while they were doing a job. It is not indexed by anything, and there is no card in that building or any other that says who asked for it, because nobody has, and because the thing it is a log of is a relay, and a relay is a piece of equipment in a duct in a wall, and nobody has to know anything about it for forty years. Paper gets kept because paper is kept; that is the whole reason; there is no policy and no instruction, somebody in a county office in about nineteen eighty decided a record of maintenance is a record, and about forty people in that building for forty years agreed with them, and that is how a stack of paper ends up in a room with a key on a hook. It is not important that it was kept. It is important that keeping it cost nothing, and that is why it happened.** **The agreement, in the form of a condition and not a favor: nobody photographs a page that has not been through a clerk's hands first. Not one page, not a corner, not a thumb.** And it is not fair, it is old, and she has never been able to make those two words behave. They open the top volume at the front and look at nine pages: forty lines a page, a hundred and ten pages in the year they changed the form, six hundred a year, so four thousand four hundred lines a year across five years, and a name on about one page in thirty. **Bettina tells him that he is not going to get it in an hour and a half, and that the reason is not that she does not want to help him: a bound volume that has been on a shelf for forty years does not open, it opens *at a place*, and a man who knows where a book opens is not reading a book, he is looking, and looking in a store room is a thing a clerk has to be standing next to him for, and she is the clerk, and she is not going to stand next to a man from another city for an hour and a half in a room the public is allowed into for two hours on a Sunday, because a search is not a public hour.** Her day-book entry records the attendance, the times, the volume count, the pages consulted, and the visitor, and is signed. **Her refusal, in her own words: she will not write in the day book why, because a day book records movements and it is not a place to put what somebody thinks a movement meant, and if she wrote *believed this contains evidence of alteration* then in four years somebody who was not there would read it and think somebody here believed it and there would be no way of telling a clerk's record of a morning from a clerk's opinion about a morning. She will write it in a letter instead, and sign the letter, and the letter is hers and not the store's, and he can argue with her about the letter for as long as he likes.** He did not ask her to. **He understands on the bus coming home that the archive has not withheld anything and has never withheld anything, that there is nothing in that building to withhold, and that in nineteen years a woman with a key and a preservation levy and four visitors has handed over everything she has and been owed nothing for it — and that this is a much worse thing to be owed than a refusal, because a refusal is a wall you can put a door in.** Six words on the bus on his knee, and it took him two attempts. **THEY HAVE NEVER HID ANYTHING FROM US.** He walks the last mile because Ash Street is not a stop, and there are nineteen lines in nineteen hands on a wall where nobody has said a year out loud in ten days, and somewhere on a shelf three miles from a school there are four thousand lines in each of five volumes, and somewhere in one of them there is a person and a time, and the person has never once been asked what it meant.

### Chapter 75 — The Signature *(midpoint)*
Monday, day fifty-eight, Monday the third of November, in a store room in Cordell, under a light bulb in a fitting a custodian changed himself. **The request goes in at nine minutes past ten, Aitken writes the time on the register and says the eleven days start then, and then he gets the five volumes himself**, and **Wren Okonjo comes down from the counter and stands at the end of the table, and nobody opens a volume before she is in the room**, and she spends five hours standing still, which is the other thing she does. Elias asks for the autumn of ninety-eight and the volume is heavier than a book is supposed to be, and **he holds it with his right hand and his forearm and the edge of the table because his left hand has gone completely, and he asked for a chair with a low back and there was one, and nobody commented on any of it.** Forty lines to a page, four printed headings, the year a printed column and not a heading. **Line forty-one of a page for the seventeenth of October is signed at four minutes past four in the morning.** Six words about a relay cabinet, a unit in a block, and a woman's name in a small upright hand that was not a careful hand and was not a fast one. **Joon Park says the time out loud because somebody has to: *line forty-one, seventeenth of October, signed at four twelve. That is the fact and it is the whole of the fact, and I am not saying one word about the hour, because the hour is a fact, and I know what happens in a room when somebody starts being clever about a fact.*** And then Elias Rook puts his thumb on the page above the signature, **because there is a rectangle of paper there that is a different color. The field is gone. Not blank. Gone.** About two inches by one, taken out with a blade, clean on three sides and not quite clean on the fourth, **a small tear at the lower corner where the blade went the last of the way and the paper wanted to stay.** The binding is intact and nothing was pulled out. Somebody laid a blade on a bound working log and took a rectangle off it, neatly enough that a person could have mistaken it for a printing fault for about nine seconds. Underneath: a strip of the page a line and a half deep, and a partial word, and **REQUES** — four letters and a stub of a fifth. **The heading went with the field, because the field and the name on it are one object, and nobody cuts out a heading by accident.** Elias Rook stands very still understanding that **this is not a decision. This was a Tuesday: a person with a blade and a form in front of them, a field that was not applicable, a reviewer's box, and a form that asks what happened to a field.** He checks the index for four minutes because it is the only thing on the table that can be wrong. Forty, forty-one, forty-two, every line number, nothing out of sequence, nothing missing. **Nothing is missing from the index, because the index records lines and not fields. There is no column in it for a field. So a field can be taken off a page in a bound volume and the index cannot notice, and it did not notice on the day it was done and it is not going to notice in four years. That is not a hole in the record. That is what a line is.** Elias asks for the measurement to be written as a figure and not a feeling, **and then Joon Park refuses the shape out loud, once, in a room with a clerk in it: a movement book in that building with a line dated in a year that does not match the thing it records, and now a working log in the same store with a piece of paper cut out of it, and both the same year — if he writes that shape down, the annotation in two thousand and eleven stops being a record and starts being a story, and he has watched a document stop being a record twice this month and is not doing it a third time.** The annotation is on the flyleaf in a different ink: *reviewed*, a year, a unit, the field **superseded**, the field **not applicable**, signed in a block with a unit number and no name anywhere, **because a unit can sign a review and a person cannot be found inside one.** Two words in a box, about a field, on the correct day, in the correct space, by somebody who was not hiding anything — **the same shape as the eleventh field on a renumbering form in the same region nine years later, and the same shape as a box printed in about nineteen eighty-odd by somebody who thought very carefully about a field.** And Elias says it out loud, once, and does not say it again: **A SIGNATURE IS A FACT WITH A DATE ON IT AND IT IS NOT AN EXPLANATION.** Wren Okonjo finds the name in eleven seconds in a staff index nobody uses and reads it out once and shuts the book. **Hester Lund. Sixty-eight. A caretaker at a primary school on Sycamore Row, three streets away, eleven years in the post.** **Joon says she is not a villain, and that if any of them walks out of that door and says that word it will have been said by them and not by her, and she will not be in the room to be asked.** The review file is a thin folder and the covering sheet carries the response of the originating signatory **in the approved form**, and the answers are correct. **Joon Park reads it, copies out every line in his own hand — his own words for the headings, her words for the answers — dates it, reads the copy back to the room, and adds nothing**, and writes the number of the answer at the bottom, and the number is two. **There is going to be a third time. He has written that down tonight, in a room, so that nobody afterwards can say he did not know, and he is not going to be the man who makes it a fourth.** **To use the thing she signed they would have to put her name in a proceeding, and a case number on a name is a thing that cannot be taken off that name by anybody in this business, including us. We are not doing it. The book is weaker for that, and it will be weaker in a year, and he is not going to be the man who explains to a Deputy Director in October that we had a good thing and did not spend it. It goes on the schedule as a thing we saw. That is all it is going to be.** **And ten minutes later he is wrong, in the mirror of the holding number:** he thinks that because the name is not in anything, the page is safe. Four competent people in that city have read it and every one of them will one day have to describe what they saw to somebody who is not us, **and a record that cannot be taken is a record that cannot be corrected by anybody, including the people who were there.** Rusk takes the call himself at twenty past three with no recorder on the line, and neither man says the year and neither man says her name. Elias tells him the reason is not honorific and not tact — **the only way that document enters the inquiry is with a person attached to it, and the person attached to it has answered two institutions in a form and has not asked to be asked a third time — and that we cannot use it, and the reason we cannot use it is the reason you will want it for, and he would rather bring Rusk a true thing he could not use than a usable thing with a woman in it.** And Rusk: **That is fair. I am doing something with it and I want you to hear it first. You have shown me that this office's predecessors edited a book. That is not evidence about what you did. It is evidence about what I have to answer for.** Elias stands with a telephone in his right hand and a bound volume under his forearm and says nothing for nine seconds and then says it is the fairest thing anybody has said to them in nine weeks and he would like it minuted. **I am not running a trial. I accept the whole of it into the file and rule on none of it.** Send the date and the block, Tuesday morning, not today — **today the woman exists as a woman and not as a block** — **and that is the sixth true thing, and it is worth less than the other five.** And at twenty to four **Rufus Aitken comes and puts both hands flat on the trolley and says he is going to do a thing he has not done since a Wednesday in a different year, and tells them before he does it and not after, because the last time he told nobody it was nine days before anybody found out what he had done.** He reads the line out loud in the voice he uses at a counter. And then: nineteen years ago he read a page out loud to a man in interview room two, because he thought *heard* and *told* were two different men, **he was right and it changed nothing, and the file did not change, and he has not done it since, on purpose. I am doing it now because the person in the line is alive and is not in the building, and that is the difference, and I am not claiming it is enough. By Friday it will be in four books and it is going to change the file by nothing at all.** Elias puts the volume down, his right hand is shaking, and he puts it flat on the table, **and Joon Park writes down the time and not the reason.**

### Chapter 76 — The Hall Is Entitled To Be Angry
Tuesday, day fifty-nine, a depot morning and then the front room at seven. The hose holds at twenty-five minutes to seven. Inez Sato telephones to say there is a meeting at seven and that it is not about the last trip, and puts the handset down on her own end, which she has done once and never twice. **Mara Okafor catches him on the ramp and says the decision is about a person in a bed and not about a piece of paper, and that she is not going to discuss it with him first, and that she is telling him now so he cannot brief the room: Comfort Osei is moving onto the ward circuit for the four dark hours from Friday, on her husband's card, and he can say no.** She refuses his offer to carry the number in, because that is the trick and she has watched him nearly do it twice: **you hold things and I decide, and if you carry it in for me I have handed you a job and not a say.** Twenty-one people at seven. **Mara says the decision in the order the rule requires in ninety seconds: four dark hours a night from Friday on the ward circuit for Comfort Osei, on her husband's card, which is a week of his cooler, said to him before anybody else and said again so the whole room has it at the same moment he does, and he can say no.** Osei Kwarteng says no, standing up, in a quarter of a second: **not because I want her in the dark, and I gave a card away in the first week for the same reason and have not regretted it, and I am not going to be a name in a transcript reading *his wife*, with somebody checking what it cost him.** So it is batteries, and the clinic will charge, and Mara will go and be the one who asks, and would rather be told no in a room than be right in a corridor. **She then refuses the vote out loud, in the flat voice, and the reason is that there is nothing to vote on: *nobody inherits me. A vote would be a way of pretending it is a thing the room owns, and it is not.*** Nobody takes it up. Inez writes *no vote taken* and the room does not applaud. **Then Inez applies the rule on the wall to a decision of her own, out loud, and it costs her: the rule is hers to have and a doctor wrote it and she put it on this wall, and for four weeks every person in the room has used it on somebody else and not one has used it on her — and after tonight she is not the person in that building who decides things in that building.
