She decided that herself, tonight, in this room, and is not going to be overruled on it and is not going to enjoy it.** She describes the discovery in full and **then says the thing that is the batch's discipline: she has not said the year, and she wants that written down, because she wanted to say it out loud since Wednesday, nobody in the room stopped her, she stopped herself, and it is the first time in nine weeks she has wanted to say a thing out loud and not said it.** **Emil Baros puts a pencil and a sheet of the hall's paper on the table and argues with her about it: a pencil and a Tuesday, three weeks of copying our copy, and tonight he has to hold the pencil while the hall writes down a thing that makes it look like a hall that finds things, and he did not ask to be that man and is not refusing it, because he put his name to a line and a line is a line. And here is what he has got — item fourteen. The clerk wrote *items, 2*. Our copy says *items, 2*. The Office's copy says *item, 2*, singular, no comma. It is the only difference in twenty-eight lines, it is one letter, it took him three Tuesdays, and nobody in that building had read that line closely enough to know it was different. If we ever have to stand in front of somebody and say our copy and theirs are the same document, they are not.** Lena Mir says *that is good work, Emil* and he goes red to the ears, and it is the only warm thing in the room for six minutes. **Tarin Voss, from the door, with his coat on, asks the only question anybody can ask — does this make us the people who find things — not whether it is right, whether it is going to be what we are, because in a year somebody will write a sentence about this district with a noun in it and the noun is going to be *records*, and there is a version of that sentence a family gets turned out on, and he does not know how many of them it takes before the sentence is true.** Petar's number is offered and Nadi Bell says it is a number and not an answer, and gives the batch's own answer: **this hall does not find things, this hall keeps things other people have decided are finished, and the difference is which way the pressure comes from.** She has never in her life required an answer. **Elias, with his back to the wall, says the team is not going to publish it, and the reason is one sentence: a document that cannot be used is still a document that has been read, and four people have read it, and a fifth person's name is on a line in it. If you want to publish it he will carry the boxes out himself and will not say a word against it.** Inez says he has not read the rule he is standing in front of, and he admits he has not. She writes and reads it out: *the team has refused to publish a document. The hall has not been asked to agree and has not been asked to refuse.* He asks for it to go in the file. **She will not: the file is the thing under a demand, and you told me that on Wednesday.** It goes on the wall in chalk, and Petar reads it out in the morning, **and she is not agreeing with him either and has not read the document and is not going to be one of the four.** The record is **no answer given**, dated, with her name on it, and anybody who wants to say something can say it at seven tomorrow and not tonight. It is the third thing under the clerk's fourth column that nobody in that building owns. **And the thing Elias understands at twenty past eight, standing at the back of his own front room: a wall is not a wall because it is safe, it is a wall because somebody decided what goes on it, and every one of those twenty-eight lines was somebody's decision, and by Friday there will be a description of all of it written by somebody who was not in the room, and it will use the word *records*, and Tarin Voss is going to be right, and none of that required anybody to decide anything. The description started on its own. It has been running since a clerk in an orange jacket wrote a fourth column on that wall on the thirty-seventh day and did not sign it.** He says it to Joon Park at the door and Joon writes it down and adds nothing. Outside, the seal stands, the eleven meters is a length with a day on it, a man behind a wall inside it is **fifty-nine days in** and unwritten at his own request, and in four days a Deputy Director is going to decide what to do with the best thing any of them has found, and he will do it correctly.

### Chapter 77 — What I Have To Answer For
Wednesday, day sixty, the Office's inquiry room, eight chairs and eight people, and **Recorder Bernadette Locke types the first four minutes and then stops typing and just writes, which she has done four times in two months.** **Rusk asked for the inquiry room and not a conference room, and nobody said thank you, and Elias understood about forty minutes later that it was a kindness.** Elias comes in with a tin and a piece of paper with a date and a block on it and nothing else, and Rusk says the whole of the account goes in and he rules on none of it, and that he has said that three times and is not going to say it a fourth time in a nicer voice — **and he says why the room gets a different promise than the telephone did, which is the recorder.** **Counsel Renata Solano asks for the date of the annotation and the block of the unit and nothing else and does not offer it, and does not ask for a name, and gives the reason on the page: there is not one in what he handed her, and if there were one she would have to ask, and if she asked and was refused the finding would be about her.** She will write it in three sentences in the Office's words and not theirs: *an alteration in a regional maintenance record, in a store in another region, in a year not stated in this file, by a method not established, with a review annotation of a later date signed by a unit.* It is going to look thin, on purpose, and Rusk tells her to write it anyway. **Then Rusk explains the process, slowly, in the order, because the order is the entire answer.** An irregularity in a record is answered by a process: a review, a reference, a date, a finding; if the finding is that a record has been altered it is a *records management* finding, and that is about the record. **It is not about a person, and the reason is that no individual in that system had a hand in it, and if one had it would be a different act and he would refer it.** Tomas Vale says *that is correct*, and Rusk says **it is correct and it is worthless and both are true at once, and that is the part that took me two years.** In four years somebody gets a file that says a regional record was altered in an unstated year by an unestablished method and a review confirmed a field was not applicable, **and what is in that file about anybody is nothing: no name, because there is no name, no unit that can be given a meaning, because a unit is a block. There is no author.** And the sentence in that file in four years is going to be *the record was amended in accordance with review*, and it will be true, and there will be nothing in it to put in anybody's hand, and nobody will even be able to be angry at anybody in particular, **and that is not a scandal. That is what an institution is for.** Elias Rook hears a person being correct and it is the worst sound in that room. **And Rusk gives them the sentence the team has been unable to say for two months: it is the same shape as a printed box, somebody in about nineteen eighty-odd thought carefully about a field and printed a box that asked what happened to it on a form a clerk would fill in correctly and go home from, and neither has a person in it, and the reason neither has a person in it is that the design is good.** He owes them that sentence as much as they owe him the date. Joon Park writes *PROCESS, NO AUTHOR* and the recorder types it. **Then, at about half past eleven, Rusk says the thing to Tomas Vale's face, and Tomas has not spoken for ninety minutes, and the room is told to hear it in a room with a recorder rather than in a report: *you believed the truth was better for you. That is the most dangerous belief a man can hold in an inquiry. Not because it is false. Because it is true, and because it is true it will feel like a correction every time you do it and never once like a cost, and every time it feels like a correction the file gets a little more complete and a little less useful, because a man who is only ever right by being more complete will eventually be handed a question he cannot answer without inventing something, and he will not notice, because inventing it will feel like the truth catching up.*** **And Elias Rook writes nothing in the gap, because he heard the same sentence nine days earlier on a telephone in a station in this city from Constance Ilori, who is fifty-seven and was not on his side either, and neither of the two of them has ever heard of the other one — and there is no form in this business with a box for two people arriving at the same sentence about the same man nine days apart.** He says it is not advice, he has no standing and no clause gives him one, and it is a Deputy Director telling a named man what his belief looks like from about eleven weeks away. **Tomas says thank you and Rusk tells him not to thank him and to write down that he said it and that Tomas said thank you, because in October somebody will read them next to each other and they will make a very good paragraph, and the recorder types both.** At twenty past one Rusk announces two things about a report he has not written, and says before he starts which of them costs him something. **The report is the same report either way, and nothing that happened on Monday changes a word of it, and somebody cutting a rectangle out of a book in nineteen ninety-eight is not a paragraph about what the crew did.** And the thing he chooses: **the annotation goes in as a fact with a date, not as a finding, and a fact with a date can be shown to a person and a finding decides something, and he has no authority to decide anything and is not going looking for a way to get it.** Solano notes that is the whole distinction the findings section exists to make and he has just made it in advance on the record. **Rusk says it is the seventh true thing he has said and it is worth less than the other six, and that he is telling them before they can take a procedural kindness for a concession, because he has watched them do that four times in two months and is not going to be one of those four.** Then the third sitting, which was going to be in October and is not: **Wednesday the nineteenth of November, ten in the morning, in this room** — and it is the same day the team's request is due at the near end, and Rusk says it is a coincidence of calendars and **he chose it**, and wants it written down that he chose it, because in a month somebody will decide for himself whether that was a courtesy or a tactic, and it is neither: **it is the closest thing to fair I have been able to arrange in nine weeks, because you have spent nine weeks being told by my office that your request went into a queue, and the queue has a date on it, and it is the same date. I am not going to hold your answer hostage to a calendar and I am not going to pretend a calendar is the only thing I have.** Asked whether he would have taken it in October if asked, he says **no**, and wants that minuted too, because it is the honest answer and the reason the offer is worth what it is worth. **Inez Sato asks the only question she has, which is whether his office has ever in nine weeks produced a document the team could hold, and he says no, and she says *thank you, that is the correct answer, and I am not going to say it again*, and he says that was also the correct answer.** At half past three a clerk says a woman in Cordell has declined the offer of a machine and asked for a table, and Rusk will take it himself at five, and says to nobody that she is the second person this week asked for something in a form who has said no, **and he is going to stop using the word coincidence in this building.** Elias gets what he asked for, and it is a procedural kindness, and it is worth exactly what Rusk said it was worth, and it is not the thing the hall needs, and **on the bus back he understands that those two facts are not in tension, that he will have to hold both for about two more years, and that there is nobody in Havenport he could say either of them to.** A school on Sycamore Row is three streets from a store room where a book is still lying open on a trolley under a bulb, and on Friday a man who cannot write in his left hand is going to stand in that corridor and not ask a second question.

### Chapter 78 — The Third Time
Thursday, day sixty-one, the sixth of November, a primary school on Sycamore Row, half past four. **One story, built about 1958 in a hurry, corridor eleven meters, a radiator that ticks on the north side, every classroom dark and every door locked except the one with a wired glass panel in it.** Hester Lund, sixty-eight, is mopping with a frayed head and lets them in without asking who they are, and in the first minute says **she is not going to talk in there**, and makes them stand where she can see the yard, **because two of them are about to ask her a thing and she would rather not do it with a wall behind her and a window in front of her, which costs the other person nothing and costs her quite a lot.** **Elias Rook says the rule out loud, to her, before anybody asks anything: there is an inquiry with a case number on it, her name is at the bottom of line forty-one on a page in a paper book, and they have not written her name down anywhere and are not going to; the only reason they are in the corridor is that they would not use the page without asking her one question first. And then the price: *if we use the page, your name goes into a proceeding. That is the deal and there is no way around it. So we are not going to do it. It is not a favor to you. It is the price of the page, and we are paying it, and you will be poorer at the end of this conversation than you were at the start of it.*** She thanks him and goes on mopping. She tells them she has already been asked twice, and offers to describe what that was like, and Joon Park says they would like to hear it. **He asks one question, and he asks it because he is the one who asks, and that had been six weeks of practice and it was the only reason the corridor had a chance: *do you remember the night?*** And she will not fill a gap to be helpful: **which night — you have not told me which night, and I am not going to be the woman who fills in a gap, and if you cannot tell me which night then you are asking a person a question about a thing you have not looked at, and I have been asked that three times and answered it three times and it is the only question in this business I have ever wanted to walk away from.** **Joon Park does not fill it in, and that is the whole of what he did, and it took about four seconds, and it was the hardest thing he has done in nine weeks.** He gives the date, the cabinet on the wall at the corner of the yard, the run, the hour, and the line. And then **she gives it to them in the approved form** — not in her own words, in the same order and very nearly the same words, flat and reasonably quick, the way a person gives a thing she has given before: a cabinet on the wall at the corner of the school yard had been opened and worked on, she was asked to sign for it because she was the person on site and the man on the run had gone home, and she asked at the time whether her name was the right one for that column and was told it was the name on the building; she was asked whether she had observed the work and answered that she had been on site and had not observed it; she did not know the purpose; **and she was not asked to say anything about who had requested it, and if she had been she would have given the same answer again, which was that she did not know.** She says it is a form they have, with a right answer on it, and she has got quite good at it, **and she is not going to be a third kind of woman for a third institution, so they are getting the form, and if they want the other one they will have to ask her for the other one and she will not give it for a question they have not asked.** Joon Park copies all of it, reads it back, and adds nothing. **Then she says: *you are not going to ask me a second question.*** She says it is the third time in nine weeks somebody has not, and the first two were a district man and an operator's man, and she wants him to understand what it does to a person: everybody wants the second question, the second question is the one they did not write down, **and it is hanging in this corridor and it is the only thing in this business I have ever wanted to be asked and I am not going to ask you for it.** **And Elias Rook says nothing at all, which is what he has been practicing since the second week of September, and it is the only thing in the chapter that is not ordinary.** So she tells them about the vans, and she did not have to, and nobody had asked. **Two vans in the yard, one with its interior light on; she looked out, thought somebody had come out to lock it and left it on, went out and locked it for them, and did the second one too because it was the same night and she was already out. That is all it is. It does not name anybody. It does not mean anything. In thirty years of being the one who is here when everybody has gone home I have never told anybody that, and there is no reason in the world for me to be telling two strangers from another city in a corridor.** Joon Park writes it down in his own hand, stops, reads what he has written, and looks up. **He will not write what anybody thinks it means — the fifth time in nine weeks, and the first time he has had to do it about a pair of vans — and he says: we do not know what that was. It could have been a heating pipe and a night cleaner. It could have been nothing and a tired woman with a key ring. It could have been the most important thing anybody has said to him this year and he does not know that, and if he writes it down as though he did, then in four years somebody will read a sentence he made out of a woman's two vans and believe he found it somewhere, and it will be the first document he has ever put in a book that he could not show anybody where it came from.** She says he is the third one, and that the other two did not say that, and one said *of course we understand*, and **she would rather have what he said than what the other man said, and she is aware that what he said is only a rule and not a kindness.** He says it is only a rule. **She says that is why it is worth something.** At twenty past five he says thank you and she asks them to walk out through the yard because the front door sticks after four. On the bus he works out what happened: **she had not refused him and she had not helped him, and it is easier to be in a room with a person like that than with one who is refusing you and a great deal easier than one who is helping you, and the reason is that neither of the other two is finished. A refusal is a door. A favor is a debt. A woman who answers in a form and then tells you about two vans has not given you a door or a debt.** He has spent nine weeks being extremely careful about who he is owed something by, and this was not a favor either. At half past nine the hall has four dark hours two hours off, Petar is reading the board, there are three questions on it and none answered, and on the front-room table there is a tin with a band round it twice and a notebook in it with six words in Elias Rook's handwriting — **they are his, from the bus on the Sunday, and the notebook they are in is the one in the tin** — that nobody in that building will ever be able to use, and about two inches by one of nothing at all.

### Chapter 79 — What You Do With It
Friday, day sixty-two, Ash Street. **He has no slot at the depot that Friday, because he was four hours late to the one before it and the foreman told him a slot would not be held twice, and he said out loud that he would not be the man who argued with that, and so he was not there.** He walks over at ten past six on his own time with the week's inventory sheet, because the inventory half of the job is his and a hose is not, and R. Dunleavy takes it at the bay door, reads it standing up, writes two figures on it in his own hand, gives it back and says nothing else. **Twenty-six people in the front room at seven**, which Inez Sato arranged with one line on the pad: *the thing we found, seven o'clock, everybody who wants to be angry about it.* **Nobody wrote the year on the pad. Nobody has written the year in that building in eleven days.** Joon Park lays out four options and refuses to give an opinion. **One: record it, in a hand, in a book, on a shelf in this building — and in three years somebody serves an order on a residents' body that cannot produce a file, and this time there is no wall to put it on, because a wall is a wall and a book on a shelf in a corridor is not, and an order does not care what a book is. Nadi Bell: that loses it. Two: publish it — and Inez Sato says no without letting anybody finish the sentence, because a description of a page that carries a name is a document about her, and in three days a Deputy Director's clerk has got a document about a school caretaker and there is no line on it for her to refuse on. Somebody asks for the rest of it and she says nobody in this room is going to say the rest of it and she is not going to help. Three: hand it to the Office — which is a good outcome and a fair outcome, and becomes a fact in a report with a case number on it, and the case number is about us, and from Friday afternoon a document four people read in another city exists inside an inquiry about this street, and there is nothing anybody in that room can do about it ever again. Four: give it away**, and nobody had thought of four, and he thought of it at ten past six that morning while a man wrote a figure on a sheet and said nothing, and the reason was a tin on a table and a woman with a rubber band round it twice. **To the local-history room, which has already got a copy of their copy and a catalogue card with their name on it and a condition on it: not a document, a description of a page, in their hand, with no name on it, put in the box, a card on the shelf, and that is the last anybody ever does with it.** Ru Sabar puts her card down — back on the board since ten past six, laundrette running since a quarter past four — and will not let it be called safe. **I am not saying it because of a document. I have watched four of you work for nine weeks on the assumption that there is a place in this building where a thing can be safe, and I have kept that laundrette for six weeks on a condition, and a condition is not a safe place, a condition is a thing you can break, and I break it myself every night at ten past six and that is the only power I have got. So I am not saying no. I am saying that somebody ought to be honest that a loose thing in a county library is not safer than a loose thing on Ash Street. It is just further away, and further away is a different kind of cold.** Lena Mir asks what is actually different. **Inez Sato gives the batch's smallest true sentence: the hall's money is a card, and when we say a hall keeps something loose we mean we have it, anybody may have it, and nobody can prove we have it, and that is a bad word and it is ours — and in a Deputy Director's vocabulary the same object is *an uncontrolled document*, and that word is the correct one, and she wants it printed on their line on purpose so that in five years anybody reading the page knows what kind of thing it is, and so nobody comes in here in ten years and tells them that what they have is uncontrolled in the way that means *careless*.** Tarin Voss says that is a good sentence and she says she knows and does not thank him, and he has never been thanked for a sentence in that building. **Joon Park's custody schedule has nineteen items and every one names a building and a holder and it says *not in this building* four times, and it is the only document anybody in that room will still be able to use in five years.** He gets a pen out, stops, puts it down, **puts his left hand flat on the page to hold it open and turns the schedule to face Elias, and says: write this one, I have got my hand on the book and I cannot write and hold it at the same time, and I am not being romantic about this, it is logistics.** Elias says that is a true thing to say out loud and will be written down in a book that is not that book, later, when he is not in a room with twenty-six people. **He writes item 20 with his right hand on his knee in about ninety seconds and reads it back before he turns the book round: one item, no copy and none obtainable, two pages, hand of J. Park, describing a page in a paper working log held in a regional store, a field removed from it, and a review annotation of a later date signed by a unit; no name of any person appears; not a record; location, a local-history room in the county seat, in the box, on the shelf, under the written consent of the people who wrote it; address, none; given, not lodged; not to be recalled; not to be produced on request by anybody, including us; holder, not held; envelope number 413, entered in that room's own catalogue by somebody who was asked.** **It is the first line in nineteen with a location and no address, and he has written *holder: not held*, which is a thing he has never written in a column with a heading on it, and in five years somebody will read it and think it is a mistake, and it is going to be the only true thing on the page, and he is not going to be here to explain it.** Elias says he is going to be here, and Joon says he is going to be here and is not going to explain it. **Elias understands at ten past eight that he has done the most useful thing he has done in nine weeks and it was writing down somebody else's line**, and that nobody in the room will make a speech about it, and that a man who had spent six weeks being the one who holds a tin, and who had refused a bus fare on Friday morning because he was the man who holds the tin, had just been, for about ninety seconds, the man who writes. He does not enjoy it and is not proud of it, and writes the date under it at one in the morning with four of the dark hours gone and Petar's lamp somewhere ahead of him in the corridor. **At half past ten the telephone on the shelf that only receives rings, and it is Cordell, and it is Della Marner, who has rung twice in nine weeks and both times about her husband: *did you find something* — and he says there is a page, it is the best thing anybody has found in nine weeks, it is a thing he is not allowed to use, and there is a woman in it, and he will not tell her the name, and he would tell her in about a year if the woman had agreed to it, which he doubts. She asks if it was about him. No.** And then: **I want you to understand that as a woman who has been in a chair in a building for sixty-two days waiting for a piece of paper and not as a woman who does not care. If it is not about him then it is a page about somebody else, and somebody else has already had three institutions at her, and I am not going to spend the one thing I have got on it. I have got a piece of paper. My husband is four minutes from this telephone and he comes home on Tuesday, and his name is on a wall in your city spelled the way he spells it, and that is the whole of what I have been asking anybody for since the sixth of September.** He says yes. She says he is not going to tell her his name is not in a system, and he says he is not going to tell her anything of the kind. She says *right* and that she is going to go to bed, and it is Friday, and you are a man in a corridor, and she has thought about it for two seconds and has one more thing: **whatever it is, do not put her in it. She is somebody's mother and I have been somebody's wife for thirty-one years, and I would rather you left the page alone than made it into a thing that turned a stranger's name into a sentence.** The line goes down and he stands there. He writes it in the notebook, in his right hand, in the words she said it in, and the page has a date on it, and eight pages further back there is a page with a year on it that nobody in that building can say out loud, and both are in the same book and only one is a thing anybody can use.

### Chapter 80 — Saturday, and Three Questions
Saturday, day sixty-three, the eighth of November, Ash Street, evening. **The hall has been running without him most weeks since the collapse and it says so in the first thirty seconds and does not say it unkindly.** He comes up the ramp at ten past five, four people are doing a job and two do not look up, and **Petar Lisk walks past with a hand lamp and says, without stopping: *you were in Cordell on Sunday and Monday and on Thursday as well, that is three days out of the last ten, and I counted, and I am not counting Saturday because Mr. Park went and you did not, and that is a different thing, and I have written the two things separately.*** Lena Mir is on the round, four carded draws in four hands, one of them Ru Sabar's with her time on it, the laundry running, Ansel on his frame in the corridor with a book, awake at five, which means he was awake at three, and nobody is going to say so. She tells him to sit down or go to another city, and then, a minute later, without turning round, **that Comfort came home on Friday and is on the ward circuit from tonight, it is batteries, the clinic charged forty-one dollars for two, she paid it out of the medical tin, and nobody is to tell her.** **A meeting at seven because Inez Sato wrote a line on the pad, and the line is about what is written down and in whose hand, not about what they are doing with it, and under it in a different pen she has added that they decided on Friday where it goes and tonight is what it says** — **because the four options were settled on Friday and item twenty of the custody schedule is already written, and this meeting does not re-vote it.** Nineteen people. The tin is on the table with the band round it twice. **"The record first," she says, "and then the thing nobody has ever done."** **Joon Park says the record, and says the rest of it out loud, and asks for it to be cheap: *I am not writing what anybody thinks it means. That is the sixth time I have said that in nine weeks — the fifth was the school corridor on Thursday — and the second time it has cost me nothing, and the first time it cost me nothing I noticed it and did not decide what to do about having noticed, and I have now noticed twice and I am telling the room, because a refusal that has stopped costing anything is not a refusal any more, it is a habit, and a habit is the thing where a practice goes to die without anybody voting on it.*** Nobody says anything and he says the other half: **he does not want to stop, so he has written down that he wants to stop, and the date, and that is on the page with his name on it and somebody can look at it in a year and tell him he said so, and it is nineteen seconds long.** Inez says *minuted* and writes it. **Then: the 2011 annotation does not go in as a finding** — a finding is a thing that decides something and there is nothing here anybody can decide. **So it goes in a book, in a hand, with a date on it, and the date is Monday the third of November, which is the day four people read it and the day we decided, and the two are the same day and I am putting both of them on it.** Inez makes him read it out. **It is nineteen lines long, a description and not a conclusion, and the last line of it is *no name of any person appears on the page or in this account*, and when he has finished Lena Mir says *that is well done, Mr. Park*, and he says thank you, and it is the second time she has said it to anybody in that building and the first was to a functionary on Tuesday who went red to the ears, and she is not a woman who says a thing twice to the same man.** It is two pages and nineteen lines, and it is item twenty's account. **And then Inez says she has nothing for the next part and is going to let the room have it**, and it takes about eleven minutes, and **Lena Mir says the sentence everybody has been circling for nine weeks, once, the way she says things: two boxes, one printed in about nineteen eighty by somebody who thought very hard about a field and filled in correctly in the spring of two thousand and thirty-five by a woman who told them that being competent at a form is a skill and not a virtue, and one printed in some year she does not know and filled in in two thousand and eleven by a unit, also correctly, in a box, about a field; and in between them a piece of paper two inches by one that somebody took out of a bound book with a blade, in a year she is not going to say in this room, on a night when a woman who had asked for none of it was doing the thing that made her a name in a form; and there is not one villain in any of it. Not one. Twenty-six years I have been a nurse and I have watched three people do damage they would not have called damage, and I have never once watched a person do it who thought of himself as the villain of the story, and the difference between those two sentences is the whole of what has happened to this street since the sixth of September, and it is not a comfort, and I am not going to pretend it is a comfort, and I would like somebody to write it on the wall.** **Nadi Bell finishes it: *that is a form doing its job. Both of them. They are not covers and they are not lies and they are not anybody's fault. A form doing its job is the only thing in this whole business that has never needed anybody to be a villain.*** Inez tells them to write it on the wall, and only the two of them, **because she wants two hands on it and she does not want a committee.** **Then Petar Lisk reads the board at seven, as he does every morning, and says the wall is full: there is room for about four more and then somebody has to build another wall, and nobody in that building is going to be the one who decides that, so it is going to be a thing that happens to us, and he has decided he is not going to be the one who decides it either.** He says somebody asked him on Wednesday what the first question means and he said he did not know and then said he knows the *shape* of it, and **he is going to write the shape and not the meaning, because if he writes the meaning he will be wrong by Friday.** And in small capitals, because there is not a great deal of space left:

> **NONE OF THE THREE THINGS ON THIS WALL NEEDED A PERSON TO DECIDE THEM. THAT IS THE SHAPE. I DO NOT KNOW THE MEANING. PETAR L. — I AM NINE AND I HAVE READ IT FOUR TIMES.**

He reads it twice to nineteen people, because that is what he does. **It contains no number about people and it does not answer any of the three questions, and all three of them are still on that wall in the same hand above it, and the second half of the oldest one is still a bracket with nothing under it.** Then, small, at the very bottom, in a hand nobody else in the building has, because Nadi Bell gave him the pencil and the paper and stepped back: **19 NOVEMBER. — P. L.** He reads it out and says Inez told them the Deputy Director fixed the third sitting for the nineteenth, that it is the same day the answer to their request is due, that he chose it on purpose, and that he said he chose it so nobody afterwards could say it was a trick, **and that he has written the date because we are waiting on the date, and a hall that names a date is not a hall that is looking for a thing, and he does not know what is going to happen on the nineteenth and he is not going to guess on a wall.** Nobody asked him to take it down. Lena Mir read the round sheet twice and said it was right, in the way she says it. **Outside:** a town that lost a tunnel in September doing its ordinary evening in a district where the lights go off at midnight, and a man who has been to two cities in a fortnight and come back with less on every single occasion — **two certificates, four frames, a count of nine sealed by a stranger, a form with a printed box, a movement book with a date nobody can explain, a rectangle of paper two inches by one, four lines in a unit's block in a different ink, a schedule with twenty items and no address on the twentieth, a tin with a rubber band round it twice, and the fact that a woman in a school had locked two vans.** The ward is standing, the round is on the nail, four draws are signed in four hands, a nurse got a woman home on a Friday and paid for two batteries out of a tin and told nobody, a function has a pencil and a Tuesday in it, and a wall has two more hands on it and a date at the bottom and room for about four more. **He had not built any of it and was not the reason for any part of it, that had been true since the Wednesday of the fifty-third day, about nine days ago he noticed he no longer found it a loss, and on Saturday morning he noticed he had stopped noticing, and understood that this was the last thing the week was going to pay him, and that it was a very small thing, and that he was going to be all right.** Then he goes and reads the board, because there are three questions on it and the second half of the oldest is still a bracket with nothing under it, and somebody nine years old has written the shape of all three at the bottom of a wall in a hall where nobody can say a year out loud, **and it is going to be read again at seven in the morning, by somebody, out loud, twice.**

### Chapter 81 — A Line In A Form
Sunday, day sixty-four, the ninth of November, Ash Street. **In the second week of September Inez Sato wrote a consent card for the ward out of a district nurse's pad, laid it out in rows, and there has been a wooden box of four of them in the front room since the first week of October.** Elias Rook reads one from the top to the bottom with a pencil in his right hand, which is the only hand he has got, and stops at the last row: **CONSENT ENTERED ON BEHALF OF A RESIDENT UNABLE TO GIVE IT — signature of the person entering it, printed name, and the capacity in which it is entered.** "Whose is that." "That is mine," Inez says, "and I have been waiting ten weeks for somebody to ask me about that line, and I would have told you it was mine anyway, so do not make a thing of it." **The row has been filled in once, by Lena Mir, for Mr. Duarte in October, with a ward number in the capacity box and nothing else, because there is no relationship you can write and no capacity that fits.** The ordinary Sunday: four carded draws in four hands, one of them Ru Sabar's with the time on it; the laundry; Ansel on his frame; Petar Lisk reading the board at seven and asking nobody for anything, which is his function and which nobody in that building agrees with. **Cliff Marner came up on a board on the thirtieth day at twenty past eight in the evening at fifty-one pounds; it is thirty-four days since, and he is eleven minutes away in a bed in Cordell, and his wife is at a desk on the ground floor of the same building and has been there since the Saturday this all started, and it is sixty-four days.** The man behind the wall is sixty-four days in inside a sealed tunnel and nobody goes near him. **He rings the inquiry's number at eleven twenty and a clerk says the office is closed at the weekend, and he says *it is a question and it is not a request*, and Rusk comes on and says *ask*.** Three answers: **yes, the line is printed, it is on the current set and not a superseded one, and it was added in the winter of 2003 — form number DS-4, Revision C, issued the fourteenth of January two thousand and three, a consent line, the last row, put there by somebody thinking about the shape of the page and not about the argument.** The revision letter is in the forms and returns file set and **carries a form number, a date, a reason in one printed sentence and a district, and it does not carry a name**, and Rusk cannot say who asked and says he has looked twice, once by himself and once with the clerk who went and looked on her own, and would like it minuted that it was not withheld. **He cannot produce the form**: a form store is forty drawers and a drawer is not a document, and there is no instrument that compels anybody to open one for a man without a case number, and the team has one and it is the wrong one for a form. He can have a clerk read the revision letter out and give a copy of the letter as a copy. **Then he says the sentence he said in a store room in Cordell with the nouns changed — you have shown me that this office's predecessors typed a line; that is not evidence about what you did; it is evidence about what I have to answer for — and nobody argues.** Joon Park, on the extension, says he did not argue and wants it written down, and says he has been waiting since the third of November for a sentence worse than the one he already had, and does not know yet whether that is a comfort or a warning, and writes both down and chooses neither. **Elias's cost: the thing he has been fighting for nine weeks is a sentence somebody typed in a hurry thirty-nine years ago, and that is worse and not better, because a sentence can be argued with and a hurry cannot be located.** He writes two lines in the notebook: *The line is printed... There is nobody in it. I have been fighting a sentence.* and *A sentence can be argued with. I have nothing to argue with and I have to go to a hearing tomorrow week with nothing to argue with... and I am going to have to be careful that I do not start trying to put a man back in it so that I have something to hold on the way in.*

### Chapter 82 — The Night It Was Invented
Monday, day sixty-five, the tenth of November. **A clerk of the forms and returns unit reads the revision letter out on the telephone twice, in the order it is printed**: DS-4 Revision C, issued 14 January 2003, superseding Revision B; one line added, a consent line for a subject unable to give it; **no other field altered and no field removed**; the printed reason *no field was available on this return for consent entered on behalf of a subject unable to give it*; requested by District 9; signed Forms and Returns in a block, no name. **There is a covering slip and it says *per District 9, night of 13 January* and it is initialled, and she refuses to read an initial out, because an initial is a person.** "Questions get answered. Requests get queued. It is not written down anywhere and I have never seen it in a procedure." **Joon Park interrogates a document that is nobody's.** He says *I am not writing what anybody thinks it means* — the seventh time in ten weeks — and explains why he uses it: four questions at a counter, what is it, where is it, who holds it, and who put the name on it, and underneath them Finding 3, which assumes a document has a holder and a subject and somebody who can be shown to have made an error about one of them. **This has no holder. A file set does not have a custodian. A form store does not have a custodian. A revision has no author, no requester and no victim, and a document like that cannot be impounded, produced, corrected, or handed to a person in a room and pointed at, and it is the first document in nine weeks he did not know what to do with.** And then: on Saturday he told nineteen people he wanted to stop, and he has been waiting to find out what he would do when it mattered, **and what he did was make up a fifth question, on the spot, in a corridor, and he has never done that in nine weeks.** **They go to District 9 Service Office, 118 Ninth Street — a municipal counter, a fan heater, a wall of forms in plastic sleeves, and Mavis Toomey, twenty-two years at that counter.** He tells her what he wants before she decides. **She will not show them the book.** She opens a cupboard, finds the 2003 volume, reads one line out loud with her voice not changing: **13 JAN 2003 · MON — OFFICER ON DUTY: *(a signature)* — 02:41 — SITE 4, GATE STREET — INJURED MALE, UNCONSCIOUS ON ARRIVAL, AMBULANCE 22:10. NO SUBJECT SIGNATURE OBTAINED. RETURN COMPLETED BY DUTY OFFICER AND SENT TO DISTRICT OFFICE 06:50. RELIEF ON SITE 04:15.** A night book records what was dealt with and who dealt with it; it is not a story. **Joon Park writes the signature out in full and does not say it and does not read it back, because reading it back at a counter puts a name in a room, and Elias Rook reads the two words off the page instead because there is no other hand he can read with.** He asks *was he good at it* before he knows he is going to, and she says she was front of house, he came in on nights for eleven years and he was never anything to her. **He does not ask the second question**, and when she asks what the second question is he says he has got a rule and is not explaining it at her counter. Outside, **Joon refuses a meaning out loud, at the door, and it took a second and a half, faster than any of the other six, and he asks for that on the page: he is not going to write that he was competent, because in forty years somebody will read a phrase he made in a municipal building and believe he found it in an institution's file, and the first refusal of a practice has to be done somewhere it costs the person, which is why it happened here, in a building that is not his, in front of a person who did not ask him to be careful.** Mavis Toomey gives him, unasked, the sentence the batch does not use: **he would not have needed to write that if the form had a line on it.** It goes in a notebook and stays there and nobody on Ash Street ever hears it. **Elias's cost: there was never anybody to be angry at, and a villain is an address, and there was nowhere to put it either — a night book cannot be given to a county library, cannot go in a report, and cannot go on a wall.** Cliff Marner is coming home from Cordell on the Tuesday, and there is no designation for him anywhere and the team is not going to invent one. Both men did a small defensible thing in a hurry, thirty-nine years apart, and neither had anybody to hand it to.

### Chapter 83 — Seventy-One, And A Counter
Tuesday, day sixty-six, the eleventh of November. **The hose holds and the 06:30 slot is his and it is the third Tuesday running that he has been on it at twenty-five minutes to six, and R. Dunleavy has worked out that a man who turns up four minutes early and says nothing is a man who is not going to be asked, and has not asked.** He is going to Kessler Hardware, 112 Ninth Street, four doors down from the district office, sign original since about 1971, because the records room door on Ash Street has not latched since October and is held with a folded blanket. **He had the folder under his arm when he came in and had decided twice on the bus to leave it next door, and did not put it down, and could not afterwards work out whether that was a decision or just a thing he had not got round to.** A boy of nineteen on a ladder and a man on the far side of the counter with a badge on an apron string that says **D. PREWITT**, putting the returns away. **Elias says who he is and says he is not here about anything the man has done, and is told about the tape, and says he is not here to ask a question, and gives the whole of what he is here for including the error he makes, and is told he has just mentioned the thing he said he would not mention.** Then the turn: **"I have got a night in January two thousand and three in a book in a municipal cupboard and a signature in it. I am not going to show it to you and I am not going to say the name on it. And I have to tell you the second half of that, which is that what I have just said to you is not true."** The signature is a man's and he has known since yesterday and he let a man of seventy-one believe there was nothing here about him, because he found out on Monday that a man in a hurry is not a villain and he did not want to be standing in front of the reason while he said it. "That is the whole of my dishonesty. It took about eleven seconds, and it cost me nothing, and you are entitled to know it, because you had already decided on your own that a man who comes into a shop talking about a form in a municipal cupboard is a man with something, and I have just made your decision correct." "You did not have to tell me that." "No. I did not." **Duane Prewitt tells him, unasked, that he does not remember and has been telling people that for twenty years, and that he remembers the floor: he went down on it twice, and he thought at the time that there was not one thing on that floor anybody could have written down, and if he had to leave a man to go and look for a box on a piece of paper then whatever he wrote afterwards would be going to be about him. "That is the whole of what I have got. Thirty-nine years and a floor and about nine minutes."** Elias does not ask the second question, and says he has got a rule and it is not a good one, it is just a rule. **Joon Park, on a bench outside for an hour: *Nine and nine. The district that asked for the line is District 9, and the ward is the Ninth Ward, and the district office is on Ninth Street, and the shop is four doors down. I have been sitting on this bench for an hour thinking about three nines in two hundred yards of street and I am not going to tell you what the three nines are, because I do not know, and a man who finds a meaning in a street name has found a coincidence. Nine and nine is not a reason for anything. It is an address.*** And then the sentence that goes on the page: **he is seventy-one and he works three days a week and he is not a resource; the team has no standing over him, no line to refuse on, no way to give him a card, and no document that could put his name in a proceeding; he is a person we went to see, and that is the end of it, and anybody who writes him down again is going to be doing the thing this whole volume is about.** He comes up the ramp at ten past six and there is a hospital car at the curb and **Cliff Marner is home, thirty-six days out**, and he asks for four things on a card — *home*, the time, the date, and **not his wife's name, because she is forty minutes away and has been at a desk since the sixth of September and is not a field on a card** — and Petar Lisk writes it and initials the time.

### Chapter 84 — The Reporting Word
Wednesday, day sixty-seven, the twelfth of November. **The Office answers a question with a person, and the person is Counsel Renata Solano, forty-seven, twenty-six years in the building, and she says so in the first minute in a ground-floor records room on the Meridian with four chairs in it and a window onto a car park, and has an office two floors up and has chosen not to use it.** "The Deputy Director does not write the words. I write the words." Joon Park asks one question, drafted four times on hall paper and stripped of *who* and *why* on Sunday night, because a question with a *why* in it is a question about a person and this one was not about a person: **what is the last word of the Office's own covering letter *for*. Not what it means. What it is for.** The answer: **a report's words are either reporting words or finding words; a finding word decides something and therefore needs an author who can be spoken to, and a report that swaps them is not badly written, it is unusable; a reporting word does not decide anything, and the reporting word is *error*, because an error is a thing that happened and gets put right in the ordinary course, and it does not say who did it, not because the Office is hiding one but because a thing that happened is not a person.** She gives the reason the word was chosen: somebody in a forms office in about nineteen eighty-odd, doing a job, giving a clerk in a district office a way to close a discrepancy between two pieces of paper without the clerk having to have an opinion about anybody. **"A thing which is a thing that happened and gets put right in the ordinary course is exactly what a person is not."** And: **the form is not lying about the error.** The discrepancy is real, the roster says a man clocked on at four minutes to six, the equipment list does not have him, that is a real difference between two true documents, and reporting it as an error is accurate while reporting it as a cause or as misconduct would be a lie. **"The form is the only thing in this business that has never lied to anybody, and that is not because anybody in this building is honest. It is because a form cannot do anything else. It can only carry what is printed on it. That is what makes it useless to you and useful to me, and I have watched four good men get that wrong in the same week and one of them was me."** Then the flat voice and the window: **there is nobody in that building they can be angry at, and she would like them to stop looking, because searching is now the most expensive thing they do; she has written findings about people for sixteen years and could tell you in ten minutes which ones were people; in the last four years she has written about a box, a field, a unit, a form and a word and could not tell you in ten years which of those five a person is; and the absence of a villain is not a mercy, it is the finding, and the people paying for it are in a corridor on Ash Street.** She refuses the second half of his question, says the gain question belongs to a man in a basement whose title she could not tell you, and that if you want the answer you are asking the wrong office and she has been saying the correct sentence for sixteen years and it has never got her out of the conversation. **On the curb, Elias understands that he had wanted a motive for nine weeks and is going to get a shape, and a shape is harder to argue with because it has no adjective in it and no man to call afraid, greedy or obedient.** "There is nobody in it." "There is nobody in it," Joon Park agrees, "and I have now written down four separate things that say so in four different hands in three different cities, and I am not going to write the fifth one today, because a man who keeps writing the same sentence in more places is not being thorough, he is looking for a version of it that somebody will accept."

### Chapter 85 — The Hall, Told First
Thursday, day sixty-eight, the thirteenth of November, and **there is no depot on a Thursday**, which is not a gift, it is the shape of a man's week when a man has had a job taken off him. **Mara Okafor says a decision in ninety seconds at ten past seven, in the order the rule requires, without explaining the rule: Mr. Duarte has a clinic appointment on the twenty-fifth of November at ten past two, two hours in a car, a wheelchair on a ramp that is not at this building, an examination outstanding since October; she needs a driver for two hours, fuel and a card; and Lena Mir is going to sign a form at the clinic because he cannot hold a pen and hold his shoulders at the same time, and the room is being told that before it happens and not at the counter with a pen in her hand. He can say no to any of it including the examination, and she has not taken a vote.** Ansel Duarte, from the corridor: *I am not voting either and I am not being asked, and if you had asked I would have said no to the vote before I said no to the driver, and you knew that, and you did not ask, and that is twice in four weeks.* "Do the appointment." **Then Elias puts the tin on the table and says the thing that is hard: he wants to say the finding in the words it was said in, to the people affected, before it is acted on, and he cannot use the rule to do it, because the rule says any offer, any boundary, any refusal and any condition somebody is asked to perform before they give anything, and a document is not any of those four things, and he has been trying since Sunday to work out how to use a rule on a thing it was not written for and cannot.** He gives the finding anyway: the Office's own return has had that line since 2003, added after a night, no name in it, a man in a shop on Ninth Street who was on that night, and they are not going to use his name, and there is no form in the world that asks a man of seventy-one for his eleven minutes. **Emil Baros proposes a standing item on the pad — forms, once a fortnight, not to do anything, just to know — and Nadi Bell names what it becomes (a heading, a date, a line for what was found, a line for what we do about it, a signature block) and Ru Sabar says a condition is not a safe place.** Inez Sato: **"The hall is not going to hold a meeting about a form. A hall that convenes itself about a form is a hall that has started a process."** And she says the whole of her own decision before she makes it, because she put the rule on the wall in the third week and has never used it on herself: **what she is deciding is what this building's mouth is allowed to do, and after this morning she will be the only person on that street who knows a finding that nineteen people know and nobody will be able to check it, and that is a bad way to live and she is choosing it.** And the two lines that go on the wall: **the hall has not been asked to agree or to refuse, and the hall does not get to have not known and it does not get to have not asked, and one of those is going to cost somebody a feeling and she does not care.** The driver is settled in four minutes: Osei Kwarteng takes the Thursday and not the twenty-fifth because the twenty-fifth is on the feeder rota, and Ru Sabar takes the twenty-fifth as a swap against a night and not a favor. **And then Ansel says the thing nobody can put back: a hall that knows how forms work is a hall that can be described, in one sentence, accurately, by people who do not like us — not a lie, not a villain, a sentence — written by somebody who was not being cruel, and he is not frightened of the Deputy Director, he is frightened of the sentence, and the sentence is fair, and he would like it minuted that a man on a frame who cannot walk to a hospital noticed it before any of them.** Nobody manages it. **Tarin Voss, on the step, refuses the form watch and says a pad is a file that has not been given a stamp yet, and says that since *the hall does not get to have not asked* is on a wall, somebody in that building is going to do the asking, and it is not going to be Elias, and it is going to be him, and he wants that written down while it is still only a warning.** **And Elias goes up the ramp at twenty to nine wanting to ask Inez for the finding, understands that she would give it to him, and stops — because that is the third time in a month he has been on the wrong side of a wall, and this one has no door in it, and a wall is not a wall because it is safe, it is a wall because somebody decided where it goes, and she put it exactly where it belonged.**

### Chapter 86 — Who Benefits *(midpoint)*
Friday, day sixty-nine, the fourteenth of November, **a depot day without a hose**: the Friday slot was returned in a letter four lines long, the inventory half is his, and R. Dunleavy takes the sheet at the bay door, writes two figures, and says nothing at all, which is the third Friday and is now a habit on both sides. He is back at half past four and there are already three people in the records room: Joon Park with the custody schedule and the tin, and **Tomas Vale, who has been at the table since two in the afternoon with a copy of the revision letter and about eleven pages of his own handwriting, and has not said anything to anybody for two and a half hours.** Joon Park asks the question the whole of this business has been walking in a circle for ten weeks — *what does a system gain by having fewer contradictions in it* — and says he is not going to ask a fourth person, and Tomas Vale says *sit down, and do not write anything for a minute, because I am going to say it once and I would like it said in a room with three people in it and not typed.* **The answer, in his words, said once:** a branch is a place where what happened and what somebody said happened do not match; one report says one thing, the other says another, and the size of the difference is the size of the contradiction. **"A deleted branch produces fewer contradictory reports."** He says that is a count and not a moral sentence, and that nobody in the room argued with it, and that he has been waiting since the fourth of September for somebody to put a person in it. **The job of the network is to suppress contradictory reports; that is not what it does, that is what it is for; and a network built for that is measurably safer with fewer of them in it — not safer for us, safer — and that is the design target, and he has spent thirty-one years being taught to read that number and read it correctly on Monday afternoon.** So nobody has to decide anything and nobody has to be careless: **the machine is pointed at a number, the deletion takes the number up, the number is the safety, and the machine is right about itself.** And it reports all of it with one word, and the word is *error*, because an error is a thing that happened and gets put right in the ordinary course, a contradiction is a thing that happened and gets put right in the ordinary course, and if the loss of a branch is reported as an error then the person it happened to does not have a word in the report at all, **because a person is not a thing that gets put right, and a thing that gets put right has no name in it, and that is the whole grammar of it and the grammar is correct.** Then the second half, and he asks for it to be written down that he said the whole of it once: **he has spent nine weeks telling that hall that the number of decisions in a corridor is the number of people who get hurt; that is not wrong; it is the most correct sentence he has said in ten years; it is the sentence the machine is built to act on; he has been saying it since he was thirty-four and has never asked who was counting, and did not ask on Monday either.** "Do not make a speech at me." **Joon Park takes his hand off the notebook, which is the first time in ten weeks anybody in that room has seen him do that with a document open in front of him, and says he is not going to do the thing he does: he went to look for the meaning of what Mr. Vale has said and there is nothing there to refuse, no shape, a count and a design and a number and a word, all four of them on paper somewhere with no person in them and no inference for him to decline to draw, and he has never in his life had nothing to refuse and he does not know what he is supposed to do with his hands.** Then the thing nobody can say: **Elias could not say the next sentence, and neither could anybody else, and the reason is not grief — the next sentence was going to be a question and there was nowhere to put it. He cannot say *so the Office is wrong*, because the answer says it is not wrong, and Rusk told him on a telephone that being right is not the same as being safe. He cannot say *so we take it to a Deputy Director*, because it is not evidence about anything the Deputy Director is doing. He cannot say *so we tell the hall*, because the hall decided on Thursday that it does not convene itself about a form, and a hall handed a reason is the fastest way there is to turn nineteen people into a committee. He cannot say *so we give it away*, because there is no person in it to give it away to. He had got it, and it is a true thing, and there is nothing to do with it.** Inez Sato comes in at ten past six, does not sit down, says **you are not giving it to this hall**, and gives the reason on the page, and says she is not deciding it because it is dangerous but because a hall that is handed an answer it did not ask for has been made into something it refused to be. Tomas Vale goes and stands in a corridor. **And the batch's second error is on the page: Elias tries the answer out against the week and it comes out cheap, and it is lighter than anything he has carried since September because it weighs nothing at all, and he understands at half past six that a count with no name in it is the lightest object in the world and can be carried by anybody and fits in a coat — and that he believed, on Friday evening, that because there was no villain in it there was nothing in it to weigh anything, and that was wrong, and the reason he did not know it was wrong was that he had spent the afternoon being pleased.**

### Chapter 87 — The Fear, Not The Anger
Saturday, day seventy, the fifteenth of November. Petar Lisk reads the board at seven and says the wall is the same and he is not going to write anything new that morning; twenty-three people, the most on a Saturday since the second week of October; the three questions and the date four days away. **Inez Sato changes her mind in about thirteen hours, says the change in her mind out loud, and asks for it to be minuted, because her second reason from Friday night has got worse overnight: somebody has to be the only one who knows, it is going to be her, and she will get used to it in a fortnight, and that is how it works on anybody.** Then she tells them in eleven sentences, standing, with no meeting and nothing on the pad and no form — the Thursday decision stands, there is no meeting, no line on the pad, nothing to sign and nothing to vote on — and says why she is not keeping it, and leaves without closing the door. **It was not said in the room: seven of the twenty-three are handed it in a corridor over about fifty minutes, one at a time, and Elias Rook does four of them and refuses the fifth because Petar Lisk is nine, and the seven are named on the page and it was not written down anywhere which seven.** **The hour is the loudest thing on that street in nine weeks and it is not an argument.** Nadi Bell says the two kinds of safe and says the hall picked the wrong one in the first week and has been getting away with it. Osei Kwarteng says his wife is asleep next door on a battery the clinic charged forty-one dollars for and that he has spent nine weeks being glad about a thing this room has just explained to him. Comfort Osei, home eight days: *then it is the safest building in the city and I am the most expensive thing in it* — and somebody laughs, and she does not, and neither does anybody else. **Lena Mir says nothing for a quarter of an hour and then says she is not going to be the one to say the thing, and Tarin Voss says it:** he said a sentence in this room in November, it was the best one anybody has said in this building since October, and it is not on the wall, and he has never been so glad of it either, because **a hall that agrees is safer and a hall that argues is a hazard, and that is not a slogan, that is Friday evening, that is a count, and Mr. Vale has been doing that job for thirty-one years and is right.** This hall argues — about the laundry, the round, whose card, whether a thing on a wall is a fact or a feeling — and Lena has voted no four times and never explained and nobody has asked her twice, and Mr. Baros's weekly argument has been better than his about half the time, **and they are the most argumentative building on this street and that is the only reason any of the last eleven of them are alive.** And the safe version of them is the version where they stop. **"I am not angry. Angry is a thing I know how to do and I would like to be allowed to do it. I am frightened, and the fright is correct, and I would like somebody to check the fright and tell me it is not correct, and nobody is going to, and I have had it since eleven o'clock last night and I cannot put it down."** "Somebody check it." "I have checked it. It is correct. That is the problem with checking it." **Ansel Duarte, from the corridor, on the twenty-fifth:** the arguing is the appointment, Mrs. Sabar is driving and Lena is signing, and that is what the hazard is, and it is his legs, and he would like somebody in that room to feel slightly bad about how comfortable they find it. And he says out loud that Nadi has a movement record in a file in the next room in a past tense, so that nobody can later say the hall was quiet about it, and he does not say anything else about it, and Nadi says thank you. **Cliff Marner, home four days, forty days out, says nothing until a quarter to ten:** you have found out how a machine works and I have been asking for ten weeks for somebody to write a word about me on a piece of paper; **I am not angry either and this morning I have got a worse thing and I do not know what to do with it** — somebody somewhere got told, on a form, in a word, that one of us is a smaller contradiction than the other one, and he would like it put next to his name on the wall and not explained, and he knows what he has asked for, and Mr. Lisk can write *no* up there. **Petar Lisk then writes the shortest thing on the wall — A HALL THAT AGREES IS SAFER. THAT IS WHAT WAS SAID. I AM NINE AND I DO NOT AGREE AND I AM WRITING IT DOWN ANYWAY. — and reads it out twice, and says it answers nothing above it, has no number in it about anybody, and is not the shape, and the chapter says on the page that nobody told him what was found and that he wrote it because a man of nineteen said the first half of that sentence out loud to twenty-three people, and a nine-year-old can hear a sentence.** **And Elias works out the error:** he had thought, and been pleased to think, that an answer with no villain in it cost nothing to hold, and the whole of ten weeks had come off his shoulders on Friday night, **and an answer everybody can carry is an answer that will be carried by the people the team was trying to protect** — every refusal the team has built has had a person in it who paid, and that weight is the only reason a nineteen-year-old can say *I do not want that* to a room of twenty-three people and be believed, **and this was a coat that fitted anybody, and frightened people reach for the thing that is true and light, and the man who said it is sixty-four years old with a job and is not one of the twenty-two.**

### Chapter 88 — One Question
Sunday, day seventy-one, the sixteenth of November, the Ninth Ward. **Kessler Hardware opens at ten on a Sunday and Duane Prewitt is one of the three days it is open, and Joon Park had established that on Tuesday from the sticker on the door.** He asked Elias to come and Elias came. **Elias says nothing for fifty-one minutes**, standing with his right hand on a rack of hose clips, while a boy of nineteen restocks the top shelf the whole time and does not come down. The question is *what did you have in front of you on that night* — no *why* in it. **The answer: paperwork and a man, and paperwork first, and he has thought about that for thirty-nine years and it still comes out in that order.** A DS-4 is not a report about a person, it is a return about a site, and it has to go back to the district by eight in the morning or the shift does not close, and that is the only reason there is a clock in any of it. It had a row for the site, a row for the time, and two printed headings with a box at the end of each — one for the man who was hurt, one for the officer. **There was no row on that sheet for a witness at all. Not a small one.** They had a witness; he was asked to sign; he said where does it go; and there was no which bit. And the man on the floor could not sign, and the officer's box is the officer's. **So he wrote on the reverse, in the remarks space where you put *no further action* or *crew stood down*, that he was entering consent on behalf of the subject, and signed and timed it, and it went to the district at twenty to seven.** And he is exact about the words: **I did not write *I consent*. I wrote that the officer was entering consent. That is a different sentence and I chose it in about four seconds and I have never been sure it was the right difference and I have never been sure it was the wrong one either.** Was it the right thing: he was on nights, he had a man on the floor and a form that wanted a signature and about nine minutes of shift, and the choice was a return with a hole in it or a return with a sentence in it; **a hole in that form said the district was on that site and nobody present could say what happened, and a sentence in the margin said somebody was standing there who could, and he was him, and he was still standing there, and the ambulance was twenty minutes out** — and he will not stand in a shop and tell you he did it for the law, he did it because he did not want to be the reason the paper had a hole in it. **And why it was not enough: *because a person can be written in by anybody*. There is no way on that form, and there has never been a way on any form he has seen, to tell the difference between me writing myself in because I was standing on the floor, and a man in a warm office in this city writing a man's name in because writing a name in is easier than writing a hole. You can put that on a wall if you want. It is not against anybody. It is about paper.*** Nobody put it on a wall. **Then he gives, unasked, the fact the batch does not use: a woman came in on the Monday, four in the afternoon, asked whether the man was all right, was told he was, said thank you and went out, and it is the only time in thirty-nine years anybody has come back to that counter about that night, and she gave him her name and he has not got it and did not write it down because at the time he did not know it was going to be the only one.** **Joon Park writes it and then says out loud, before either of them has to ask, that he is not going to use it**, and gives three reasons: there is nothing behind it — no letter, no card, no register, no second person, and mine is the second-best version of it in this city; if it goes into a document it becomes a document about a woman who does not know she is in one, and *she never found out that night existed, she came in to ask about a man, and you do not get to keep that and also use it*; and it does not answer the question he came with. **It goes in the notebook, not on a wall and not in a file, and he wants the reason for each of those three next to it, because in a year somebody will read the notebook and ask why the best sentence anybody has said to him in a fortnight is not in the record, and the answer is going to be one line and he would like it to be the right one: it is true, and there is no document of it, and those two facts are the whole of the reason.** **Elias had the revision letter in a satchel the whole visit and his hand went to the strap twice and he did not open it**, and Duane Prewitt looked at the bag and said *you have been carrying it since Tuesday and you have not opened it and I would tell you to leave it there if you were a stranger and I am telling you anyway*, and Elias said it was a piece of paper and he was going to leave it in the bag. **And the chapter closes on Joon's sentence: knowing what to do with a person is not the same as being able to use one** — this is the third time in a fortnight, a refusal is a door and a favor is a debt, and this is a man behind a counter who told the truth to two strangers and asked for nothing and told them to leave it in the bag, and **he has now had two answers in three days that cannot be spent, and the two of those facts sitting next to each other in the same book might be the most honest page anybody in this business has ever written.** The man behind the wall is seventy-one days in; Cliff Marner is forty-one days out and is in a chair by a window, and a man of fifty-eight had asked on Saturday for somebody to write *home* on a card and put the time on it, and that was all he asked for and he did not ask twice.

### Chapter 89 — Two Days
Monday, day seventy-two, the seventeenth of November, forty-one hours before the third sitting. **They stand in a corridor on the second floor of an Office building on the Meridian for eleven minutes and do not go in, and it is the correct decision and it was made by nobody.** The room is being prepared: the long table out, the microphone at the end of it rather than the middle, eight chairs, a jug of water, a woman in a gray jacket going round the jugs with a cloth. **A room that has been set out for a hearing is a room that is being prepared, and a room being prepared is not yet a record, and a thing you stand in before it becomes a record is a thing you will remember differently afterwards.** Tomas Vale notes that one of the eight chairs is going to have a name card on it on Wednesday morning and that tomorrow somebody decides which one and none of those five decisions is being made on this corridor. "So we stand here." "So we stand here for about ten minutes so that we have done something, and then we go and have a coffee, and then we go home, and none of that is tactics. I would like it on the page that it is not tactics. I have watched four competent people in nine weeks make a decision in a corridor and then need it to have been a plan." **After the coffee, in a records room with two chairs and no table, standing up, Joon Park reads out the two documents the team's own request names — the 4C designation history from the 2039 consolidation with the intermediate sheets, and the holding-register page for the eleventh of May 2039 and the page either side — filed by hand at a counter in another city on the Wednesday of day forty-six, the twenty-second of October, two dollars sixty, one fare out of his own pocket, quoted at four to six weeks, near end Wednesday morning at ten o'clock, far end the third of December.** Counsel Solano: **neither is here, and here is why without asking anybody, and you should know it before Wednesday so it is not a surprise in a room — a designation history is a document about a shelf and a holding register is a document about a shelf, and neither has ever been requested by name by the body that built them, because the body that built them is a store, and a store does not know what it is for; they are there because that is where things go, and they will be produced on the third of December to a man at a counter, correctly, with a stamp, and nobody will be harmed by the delay and nobody will be comforted by the speed.** "That is not a scandal. That is what an institution is, and if you want me to say anything else about it, you are going to have to give me a different question, and there is not one." **Then Joon Park hands her the four lines in his own hand on hall paper: the date of the revision; DS-4 Revision C and the fact that the form is in a drawer in a form store and cannot be produced; requested by District 9, a unit in a block, no name on the revision letter; the word the Office uses to report a discrepancy between two records.** Solano reads it twice: **that is a good document and it is not evidence and it is not about the crew, and she will put it in as a fact with a date and rule on none of it, and this is the same procedure she has applied to everything since the thirty-fourth day and she is not going to soften it for them on a Monday afternoon.** And the second thing, which costs her: **you have brought me a form and I cannot use it, and you know I cannot use it, and you have brought it anyway, and the reason is that it is the only thing you have — and somebody in a room in four years is going to read that paragraph and ask whose form and whose incident, and there is no next line.
