Because your name is not in there, Mr. Voss, and it is not in there because this office's own practice took it out, and nobody is going to fix it, and I would like the room to see it working.** Bernadette Locke types for about nine seconds in a search box on a screen that is a filing system and not an instrument, and reads out: *No result.* **Hollis Vane, from the wall, where he had stood the whole morning without being asked: that is correct. It is the practice working. In about four years somebody is going to ask why the patient on line six is not a name, and the answer is a form this office adopted and this inquiry's own findings section carried, and used twice, and that is the correct answer and it is the worst answer anybody in this building has ever given. Nobody in the room treated it as a problem, and that was the whole of the volume in one exchange.** Rusk lets the room empty by about ten past one and says one more thing with the door open, as though it were a paragraph: **the team is not prosecuted. Three endorsements are revoked, one line is written into a form forever, and one field is created that did not exist in this city in 2042. I have presented every one of those as what it is, which is cheap, and available, and correct, and I would like it minuted that in about four years this will read as a victory, and it is a victory, and it cost less than the alternative, and every single thing in it was available to me on the fourth of October and I held it for seven weeks because holding it was worth more to this office than spending it. That is the finding too. Put it in the file. Put my name on it, because I am the one who held it.** Then he went out, and it was Friday tomorrow, and Elias Rook had nothing in his week for the first time in eleven weeks and did not know what to do with a Friday.

### Chapter 100 — The Board At Seven
Friday, day eighty-three, the twenty-eighth of November, seven in the morning. **Petar Lisk reads the board at seven and reads it out loud twice, and there were nine people in the front room at seven and it is the eighty-third morning he has done it. Cliff fifty-three days out. Della eighty-three days at a family-reception desk. The man behind the wall eighty-three days in, inside a tunnel sealed on the thirty-third day and classified in nine words, and nobody had gone to the loading lip in eleven weeks and nobody is going to and the handset line is dead, and the eleven meters is a length with a day on it and nothing else.** He reads from the top, which is the order it is in. **At the top of that wall, above everything, in a hand that belongs to nobody in the building, is a fourth column.** The heading is the Office's own printed heading, copied out in chalk by a man in a clean orange jacket who ran out of forms on the thirty-fourth day of September and wrote the last column of an impoundment return on the wall of a ward instead. Twenty-eight lines, a date and a figure against each. **And the heading says ITEMS, because that is what the Office's return says, and in twenty-eight lines there are two rooms, a laundry, eleven boxes of somebody else's evidence, a terminal, **two** anchor lamps, a corridor repeater, a corridor light, six access codes and an oxygen cylinder that line fourteen says is two, and the numbers were never going to agree because the clerk numbered the lines she wrote and not the things on the cart, and one of the twenty-eight is a child's name written out in full in a ward nurse's hand with the figure struck through it, and that is the line the wall is actually for, and not one of the rest is a thing you can carry away in two hands and the ones you can are the ones that matter least.** Underneath that column are **nineteen hands** — nineteen people who wrote, on Tuesday night, in chalk, under that column, what happened in the records room at twenty past two in the afternoon, in their own hands, in nineteen different sizes, and none of the nineteen wrote the same sentence twice. **There are nineteen hands and twenty-eight lines and the two numbers are not required to agree, and Nadi Bell said that in October about two other numbers in this building and she was right then as well.** Under the nineteen hands are two names. **CLIFF MARNER**, spelled the way Cliff spells it, in four hands, in chalk, **and as of Thursday there is a designation in a file on the Meridian with a form number and a case reference and a Deputy Director's signature on it that says *not a passenger, a person recovered from a designated site under an inquiry reference*, and it is a field that did not exist in this city in 2042. It is going to stay in chalk. Chalk is what a person wrote about a person. A printer's face is what an institution wrote about a person, and this building has spent eleven weeks learning the difference between those two things, and it is going to keep both.** **PERPETUA OYELARAN**, in Inez Sato's chalk, under a seal, with a time against it, and nobody has crossed it out and nobody is going to, and she is under Ash Street, and there is no designation for her and there is not going to be one, and she is not a rescue and she is not a deadline and she is not a row in anything. **Then the three questions, all in the same nine-year-old capitals, and the second half of the oldest one still a bracket with nothing under it, and it is going to stay like that until somebody who is not any of them writes something under it.** Then his own lines — *A HALL THAT AGREES IS SAFER. THAT IS WHAT WAS SAID. I AM NINE AND I DO NOT AGREE AND I AM WRITING IT DOWN ANYWAY. PETAR L.*; *NOBODY OWNS THE ANSWER SO NOBODY CAN GIVE IT AWAY. PETAR L. — I AM NINE AND I HAVE READ IT SIX TIMES.*; *NONE OF THE THREE THINGS ON THIS WALL NEEDED A PERSON TO DECIDE THEM. THAT IS THE SHAPE. I DO NOT KNOW THE MEANING. PETAR L. — I AM NINE AND I HAVE READ IT FOUR TIMES.* — **and the date, nine days old, because he had decided on the eighteenth, on his own account, with nobody having asked him, that a hall which puts two dates on a wall in one week is a hall starting to expect something, and he had been right about that, and about most of it, and about none of the other things.** Then, above the date, in his own hand and small, the closing line, which he wrote at about half past six the previous evening in the corridor while four people watched and none of them said anything, and it is the one this building is going to be described by, in a document, in about four years, in one sentence, correctly and without cruelty:
> **WHO BENEFITS WHEN A BRANCH IS OFFICIALLY DELETED — AND WHY DOES THE FORM CALL IT AN ERROR?**
"There is the answer under it and there is not. There is an answer to the first half. Mr. Rook has it and I have not been given it and I am not going to ask for it, because the man who said it asked in writing not to have his name on it and he is right, and because a thing nobody owns is a thing I cannot read out in a room, and if I read it out then somebody owns it. I have read the shape of it as many times as there are hands under that column and I do not know what the shape is and he does not either, and he has told me so. And the second half is why it comes back round to the form, and nobody has asked the form anything, because **you cannot ask a form a question and the form does not answer and that is not a fault in the form, that is what a form.** So I have written it down, and the wall is going to be full after this one, and somebody has to build another wall, and it is not going to be anybody in this building and it is not going to be me." He puts the pencil in his pocket. **And one more thing he is not writing up, because it is not a fact, it is a feeling: Tuesday there was a door in the north wall that is not on any of the drawings, and I was in the dry end, and my card came off the rail, and nobody came and got me. I have been thinking about that for three days and I have got to the bottom of it and the bottom of it is that somebody read my name out loud and wrote *out* against it and then did not come and found me anyway, and that is the whole of the thing and I do not know what to call it and I am not going to write it down because it is mine.** **Elias was in the room for all of it and had said nothing, and at about twenty past seven Lena Mir told him there was a box in the corridor that had to go into the records room and wanted a fourth pair of hands.** It is the size of a shoebox and it is not heavy; he has it in his right hand under a minute later. **There is a step at the end of that corridor, one step, and it is there because a door frame from nineteen fifty-odd was put in when the place was converted, and the ramp is outside by the fire door and the ramp is four meters of gentle slope to a landing that goes all the way round.** Mara is coming the other way with a cup in each hand. — Use the ramp. — I know. — It is one step. **I know it is one step, and I have got a box in one hand, and I am going to use the ramp, and I want it written down somewhere that I said I was going to use the ramp before anybody had to ask me twice.** He goes out through the fire door and round by the ramp and up the gentle slope and along it, four meters, and in through the other door, and puts a shoebox of somebody's evidence on a table in a records room at about ten past seven. **He stood there afterwards with his right hand on the table and did not make anything of it, and it was not a symbol, and his left hand was still gone and it was still gone for the rest of that year.** **Mr. Rook. You do not have to come on Tuesday.** — I know that. The slot is mine, it is on the rota, and the depot says they will not hold it twice, and that is a fact about a Tuesday and not about me, and I am going to be there at half past six. — Right, and went and did the round. **He did not ask her for anything. Nobody asked him for anything. In eleven weeks of rooms, not one person in that building had ever asked him for a promise and not one person had ever asked him to make one, and he had been braced for it on about nine separate mornings, and on this one, in particular, he was not going to be the man who asked.** The envelope to Nell is in the front of his notebook, unsent, the reason four words long, and the reason is that the road is sealed. **The road was sealed. Eleven meters of a lower service run is a length with a day on it and there is a man in it, alive, eighty-three days, who had asked them once not to write him down and had not been written down, and the tunnel was nine words and a signature, and nothing this morning touched any of that and nothing this morning was going to.** The ward is standing. Eighty-three days on batteries, lamps, seam glass, a wooden frame, an analog map, a corridor light, a laundry, a bar on a feeder any household can cut, twenty-two resident cards and twenty-eight people; it has been standing without him for most of those days and is going to go on standing without him, **and he is not on the lip and he is never going to be on the lip again.** Petar Lisk read the board at seven the next morning as well. **There are three questions on it and two names and a column that says *items* and nineteen hands underneath it and a nine-year-old's closing line, and it is still there, and nobody owns it, and that is the only reason it is still there.**

## Volume 03 — Chapter Summaries

### Chapter 101 — The Board At Seven, And Then Tuesday

Saturday, day eighty-four, the twenty-ninth of November 2042. **Petar Lisk reads the board at seven, out loud, twice, and then says there is no room left: *not for a line and not for a name. When it is finished there will be a space the shape of a shoe and whoever comes in here after me is going to think somebody was tidy.*** Cliff fifty-four days out and in the chair by the window with a blanket out of the store room; Della eighty-four days at a family-reception desk in Cordell; the man behind the wall eighty-four days in, and nobody has gone to the loading lip in twelve weeks and the handset line is dead. Seven people in the front room at seven. **Elias reads the wall: the clerk's fourth column headed *items* where it should say *people*, twenty-eight lines, nineteen hands in nineteen sizes, two names in chalk, and the three questions, the second half of the oldest still a bracket with nothing under it, the nineteenth of November still the only date, and the last line stopping three inches short of the skirting.** He reads the board out loud once after Petar, badly, and is not asked to, and does it anyway; Ru Sabar says *it is upside down* and he says *it is the order I do it in* and she says *then it is your order, not the wall's*. **The Rescue Service letter is one paragraph, dated the twenty-eighth, and it puts two facts side by side and does not join them: the extension of the forty-first-day suspension expires at the end of the twenty-eighth and is not renewed, he is restored to ordinary field duty from 1 December, the revocation of three endorsements is unchanged and is not affected, and case reference HC-42-0417 remains open and is not for the department to close. A sentence about a month is a sentence about a month.** He does not put it on a table in the front room, because a table in the front room is a thing other people read out of. **Then he goes and gets the 11 at ten past eight, with the week's inventory sheet in a satchel, on his own time, because the letter takes effect on Monday and he cannot sit still, and he is perfectly honest about that to nobody.** The eleven-minute ride: the depot, the gas holder, two bridges, the low road, the boatyards. **Tidegate: the stop fourth past the cut, two miles, a district he had ridden past four times in 1988 and never once entered.** Corrugated hoarding the length of the fence, a woman in a fluorescent vest with tape, a clipboard and a box of leaflets: *You've come on the wrong bus for the depot. — I know. — It's fine. Everybody does.* **A leaflet on good paper — better paper than anything the Continuity Office put on a table in eleven weeks — four sides, one color of ink, a photograph of a wall being built against weather: BELLWETHER PILOT PROGRAM, Tidegate works notice, Fourth Stage Tidal Defense, reading office above 6 Cutbank Row open Monday to Friday, closed Saturday and Sunday, Ref. BWP/TG/4SD/2042, and at the bottom in the same ink and the same size: *Tobias Ferrand, Regional Director.*** Forty-one thousand went out with the rates and one is on every door and one is on the school, *which is Prosser's idea, and if it's on the school then it's not a threat, it's information.* **He does not go into the reading office** — shut since six the previous evening, with three printed cards in the door about exactly how much of dealing with this happens at a counter and how much in a room. He walks instead: four walk-up blocks, a workshop strip along the cut, a boatyard, a health center repainted twice with the old name still showing, a school with nine children in it on a Saturday. **A girl of about eleven hands out sheets for Monday's meeting, and when he says he does not live here she says *then you can't come*, and that is a rule and not a discrimination and she is enforcing it correctly and he has no answer to it.** From the notice board at the yard gate: **TIDEGATE — FOURTH STAGE ALIGNMENT AREA. 1,140 addresses. 2,900 residents (works board model, 2039, adjusted twice)**, and a printed slip saying the model was adjusted twice, that a model is a model, and that anybody who wants to know what the adjustment was can write to the board's surveyor and get an answer inside twenty working days — **which is the same sentence in a different font as four things he has heard in eleven weeks, and the man who printed it has a surveyor's job and no idea what he has done.** He writes nothing down. He rides back at ten past ten, puts the sheet in the drawer, telephones the hall about a corridor light on the second landing that has been out since Thursday and that he owns, and then has the rest of a Saturday with nowhere to be in it, **which is new.**

### Chapter 102 — A Woman Who Counts Addresses

Sunday, day eighty-five, the thirtieth of November. **He gets the 11 at ten past nine because it is the only one he can afford at short notice, and because he worked out on Saturday night that a bus fare is going to be a cost of this and that he should start paying it in the cheapest currency available.** Cutbank Row on a Sunday is a street of people carrying bags; a queue out of the newsagent at number two; a woman in a vest handing out leaflets; everything open and nothing free, and the two facts sitting on top of each other. He walks the street twice looking for what an address is and cannot tell, **and that turns out to be the first useful thing he learns.** At number twenty-two the woman has her door open eight inches and a folded sheet in her right hand, and she goes in and shuts it before he gets there — **not fast, not a slam, no second sound: a door closing on him in a way that was not rudeness.** *I was going to ask whether anybody keeps a list of addresses in this street.* — *Why.* — *Because I am trying to work out what an address is in a district that is being cleared.* — *Two people. I know two.* She lets him in out of it: *you're letting the cold up the stairs for everybody.* **Winifred Alagoa, sixty-three, on Cutbank Row thirty-eight years, the flat above her her mother's before that, tea measured then tea drunk, no milk and none offered, the door at the bottom of the stairs shut, the door at the top shut, a folded towel on the sill of the one behind her that he understands an hour later.** Four hours. She does not call it a record and would not call it a document. **The sheet.** Since 2031 — eleven years this coming spring, exact date the fourth of March, because it is her husband's birthday, and the year the board put a figure on a fence that did not have the flats in it. **The line that is the batch's argument arriving in a kitchen: *there is a difference between a list of houses and a count of people. Both are a number and both go on a board. A count of people is a thing that can be out by a person. A list of houses is a thing that can be out by a house. And when it is out by a house, the house is not missing, the house is still there, and the only thing that has gone wrong is that somebody has written down the wrong number, and a person cannot be inside the wrong number.*** She watched a man with a clipboard and a headcount do the ones who answered the door on a Saturday — *and you know what a Saturday gets you* — and she has watched a board be wrong twice and both times it was buildings and both times the street was fine. **1,163 addresses. The board's model says 1,140. She goes down the sheet on a Saturday, has done since 2031, marks anything empty that was not empty in the spring, and has never got to the end of it and been wrong about a door.** Two other people have one and she will not say who: *the day I tell one person that I have got a sheet, the day somebody from a building comes and asks whether they can have a look at it, and then it is their sheet. I stopped asking in about '35.* **Then the question that costs him, which he knows costs him while he asks it: *has anybody ever asked you about it.* Eleven years. Nobody. A woman from the health center in the spring about the boiler and not one question about the sheet. A man from the board in 2034 who asked a great deal, and she gave him a number, and the number was right, and he did not come back — *that is what happens. You ask, you get an answer, and you have got your answer.* — *that is not what I meant.* — *Then you will have to ask me something else, and it will cost you the same and you may as well have had the tea.* **He does not ask for the sheet.** He asks whether he may write down the fact that it exists, in his own notebook, with the source. She thinks about it for a long time. *You may write down that it exists. That is all. And you will say where you got it, and if anybody comes with a form I want to be told there is no second copy and that it is the sheet and not a copy of the sheet. Do not write down the number. If you write down the number, then it is a thing that exists in two places, and the first place is better than the second place.* And: **you may not have the sheet, and I am not saying no to you out of meanness, and I want that in whatever you are writing, because I have been in this street thirty-eight years and I have watched about nine people be pleasant to me in a way that was a way of getting something, and this is the first one that has been the other way round.** He asks for a smaller thing than he wanted; she says she is saying yes to the smallest thing he asked for, and that she has noticed. She was born at the top of Marden Row in 1979; her husband mended boats and died in 2033; no children, and had wanted two. **He writes one line and does not tear the page out: a thing nobody has ever asked her about for eleven years is worth more to her than a thing I could have been given in a minute; I have spent twelve weeks learning what a refusal costs, and I have never once been in a room where the refusal was about eleven years of nobody bothering.** He does not underline it and shows it to nobody, and on Monday it is still true, and by Friday it has cost him something he did not go looking for.

### Chapter 103 — The Cell Above A Shop

Monday, day eighty-six, the first of December. **He is a field technician again as of midnight, in a letter one paragraph long, and on Monday morning at half past nine he is on a pavement on Cutbank Row doing the thing he has done every working morning of his life, which is to look at people first.** Cliff fifty-six, Della eighty-six, the man behind the wall eighty-six, and none of it touched the morning and all of it came with him on the bus. **Mara Okafor comes out of the health center with a box of gloves: *you are going to be here all week. — I am going to be here all week. — Then come and find me after four. I am here until Thursday, I am not a tour guide and I am not a rescuer in this district, and if anybody in that building asks you whether I sent you, I did not.*** She looks at him the way she had on Friday on the landing: *how long have you been out? — Since midnight. — Then you have not slept, and I have written that sentence down twice in my life and I am not writing it down a third time for you. Come at four.* **Number six is a shop that sells door furniture; above it, two rooms and an outside stair, the oldest and cheapest kind of arrangement there is, and the stair shakes under him. Four people, and four is smaller than he expected in a way he did not understand until Wednesday. Selwyn Anum, forty-one, stands up, puts a hand out before the door is shut, and says *Rook. You have got one hand. — I have. — Good. Do not sign anything. It saves a step.*** He said it as a joke and it was a joke and about half of it was not. *Do you want the short version or the method?* — *The method.* — *That is the correct answer and nobody gives it to me.* He unrolls about four feet of **a band: a wide margin drawn in a soft line, street by street, in three colors of ink, that is not paper that has been through a printer.** *Tell me what you think it is.* — *I think it's where the ground is carrying.* — *Correct. That is a securing band and it is the eleventh of September and it is the reason a district of two thousand nine hundred people is still a district. That is a reading. It is not a repair and it is not a prediction and it does not stop anything happening. What it does is tell you the direction force will want to travel through the ground under that line, which is a thing you can measure and a thing you can argue with, and that is the whole of why this program exists.* — *How do you get it.* — **I do not. I convert it.** Five steps, and the fourth is the one everybody thinks is a formality and is not: *before you say anything at all about the plan, say out loud that you have not told this room what you are about to do, and that you are telling them now and not afterwards. Then stop talking.* **Elias puts his cup down on the edge of the desk, carefully, with one hand, and Anum watches him do it, and says, in one sentence and then stops: that is step four of a method I have read once, in a records room on Ash Street, in eight pages that a man of sixty-four put on a table in November and said was a method and not a confession, and I have never been in that room and I have not seen the pages and I do not know who wrote them.** The woman at the counter does not look up. Anum says *Right* and goes to the kettle. **Nobody reacts at all, and Elias understands about eleven seconds late why, and it is the same eleven seconds he has been losing all autumn: the thing he recognized was not a fact about a room in Tidegate, it was a fact about a building he has been sitting in for twelve weeks, and he had carried it two miles and put it on a stranger's desk where it landed as nothing.** Step five: *stop talking and let them stop you.* — *That is the hard one.* — *If nobody stops you, you have not done step five, and you go back to step two and take it to somebody else. Nobody has ever stopped me. I have run this method in four districts since 2039 and I have had two hundred people in rooms and not one of them has ever said stop, and the reason is that they think they are being polite, and the reason they think they are being polite is that I did step four well enough that they felt told.* **Elias tries to say it three times and fails:** at half past eleven (the wrong question, answered happily at length), at about one (*but that is the same six people*, and Anum says *what is the same* and means it), and at about four, when he gets out *a room told in advance is a room and a room not told in advance is an audience* and has to put it back, because he does not know where he got it from and will not say it to a man he has known for five hours. He gives up at ten past four, rides the 11 back, writes two lines, one of them the sentence he had nearly said. **Then the Retention Schedule comes out of a drawer in a folder with a printed label, unasked, at about half past three: *You have not asked for it, and you are going to, so I have taken it out, and if I gave it to you after you asked it would be a favor and I have enough of those.*** Nineteen pages, stapled at the corner, on the same good paper as the leaflet: **RETENTION SCHEDULE — FOURTH STAGE TIDEGATE · Ref. BWP/TG/4SD/2042 · schedule of 2 December 2042 · 697 rows · Address · Structure · Occupancy class · Status.** He has spent twelve weeks in a building where a missing column was the entire case, and he counted, and there were four. **There was no column a person went in.** Then page two, in italics, four lines long: *The band is indicative. This schedule is the operative document. · Where a reading and a schedule differ, the schedule governs. · Readings are taken at intervals and are not continuous. · Current reading: 11 September 2042.* **Four hundred and twelve rows say `RETAINED`. Two hundred and eighty-five say `TRANSITIONAL`.** *Why is it dated tomorrow.* — *Because it is operative from tomorrow, and a schedule that did not carry the date it takes effect would be a schedule nobody could tell what it was for. That is not clever. That is in the guidance.* Then the conversion, in his own professional voice and in order, and **the date is the day you did it and not the day the ground moved** — said with a slight lift in the voice, because he liked being asked, about four hundred people being rehoused, as though it were a thing a reasonable person would do on a Tuesday. Then he puts the kettle on. **Elias says nothing and does not ask him a second time, and that is the growth and there is nobody in Tidegate to see it.** He reads the footnote on a bus at ten past five and again on a bench with the light going, and it does not make sense to him, **and the reason is that on the first of December he does not know there is a second reading. The second reading is two days old.**

### Chapter 104 — A Designation

Tuesday, day eighty-seven, the second of December. **The depot on the far bank runs Tuesdays and Fridays at half past six; the Tuesday slot is his and has been his since the end of October; R. Dunleavy is fifty-nine and does not do the talking part of it.** Cliff fifty-seven, Della eighty-seven, the man behind the wall eighty-seven — **and the designation signed at a quarter to eight that morning is about to make that last figure permanent in a way nobody in that building intended, and nobody is going to be told in a corridor, and a corridor is where it got told.** He is there at ten to six, which is what he does; the hose passes at nineteen hundred and forty, the gauge settles at one hundred and six, **Dunleavy writes two figures in a book in his own hand, turns the book round so the page faces the man who brought the sheet, and says nothing at all.** Twenty minutes, every Tuesday since the end of October; the Tuesday last week had been on it; **this was not a return and not a first and there was nobody there to be impressed by it. It was the first thing in thirteen weeks that had gone right in front of him and had not been him, and he did not make anything of it.** **Inez Sato, front room, ten to nine, coat on. Sit down.** Tidegate was designated at about a quarter to eight; it is on the notice board at the Meridian and on the fence at the yard; the woman in the vest handed a man a leaflet about it at about nine yesterday and did not think twice. **It is signed by a Director. It is correct. It is the thing that makes the pilot lawful, and without it the whole of what is happening in that district is a man with a clipboard.** And: **the designation is what makes you legal, and it is also the thing that puts your own line into effect.** How long have you known — *since a quarter to eight. I did not telephone you because you were asleep and because you would have come in a car and I would rather you came on a bus with everybody else.* Then the cost, said out loud before either of them pays it: **eleven minutes, twice a day, four days, and you will not see anybody in this building for four days, and you are not the only man who has anything else to be doing, and the two of you are taking a day each out of a building that has twenty-eight people in it and none of them are paid for this. Say the number out loud.** — *Eight days of travel between the two of us, and about twenty-two dollars.* — *Say it like that to Joon Park and not to me, because he will work it out and he will not ask, and if I hear it worked out I will have to look after him about it.* **And then: I am going to tell him. — What are you doing. — I am going to stand here with him for eleven minutes and then I am going to come back and tell you one sentence, and if you want to know what he said you are going to have to ask him, and you are not going to, and that is the correct arrangement and it is his, not mine.** He goes down the corridor. **Elias sits at the table and does not follow and can hear the two of them through the door because the door does not close properly, and neither of them raised their voice at any point, and he could not hear a word.** She is gone eleven minutes. *He says the designation is correct, and he says he put himself in that box in November on purpose and the box has now got a job and he is not going to be the man who takes it back out because it is inconvenient. He says you are not to come and see him about it. And he says that if you come and see him about it, it will be the one time in this whole business that he is rude to you, and he would rather have that on a Tuesday than have you turn up on a Friday.* **Elias said thank you and meant it and said nothing else, and she said *Do not,* and went out.** The letter is waiting at about five to eleven: one side of a sheet, typed, in a hand, no name beyond a clerk's. **HAVENPORT TIDAL BOARD — Fourth Stage Tidal Defense, Tidegate. Enquiries regarding the alignment area should be addressed to the board's surveyor, whose office is at Civic buildings, third floor. Requests to attend site, to observe works, or to be consulted on structural matters are not accepted and are referred. The board has no procedure for a third category and does not intend to build one during an active alignment.** **He reads it three times and cannot find a sentence in it that is unkind and cannot find a sentence in it that is anything other than unkind. There is no procedure for a third category.** He thinks about a man in a records room in November who said a thing almost exactly like it and was right; a wall with a column headed *items* where it should say *people*; a box with a form in it; **and he understands that he is in a building where the entire technique is a field that did not exist, and that the entire difference between that building and a form with a third category on it is who was standing in the room when the category was decided.** **Mara, outside the health center at twenty to one: you are not to tell me what you have found and I am not going to tell you what I have found, and I have found one thing and it is not a moral thing so I will tell you that one.** A chemist on Marden Row doing a weekly round to about forty older residents out of his own van since March, told last Thursday that a transitional address cannot be billed against the district; he has not stopped; he is fifty-eight; there is an amount of his own money that ends. *That is my question and it is a real question and I have asked it of four people this morning and got three different answers and one of them was yours is the only one that was honest. I am telling you because you are going to be in a room with people who can make it go away, and I am not, and in January it is going to be a very large thing for about sixty people and none of them will know why it happened.* — *Four o'clock is what I said and I mean it.* **The counter on the ground floor of number six:** a named observer clause, and it is the same for everybody and it is not a favor. Standing request; a name, an organization, a reason. **And then, unprompted, she tells him what kind of door he is standing in: the observer clause came in under the pilot authorization in August, it is a regional form, and it is in every cell that has one of these running, and I am not being difficult, I am telling you what kind of door you are standing in.** **Joon Park says nothing at all — in daylight, at half past two, in a voice anybody in that shop could hear, for about four full seconds — and then says that Mr. Park has noticed something about the shape of a clause and is not going to say what it is, and would like it written down that he noticed and did not say it, and is not going to explain the not-saying to a room that has not asked him for an explanation. She said *Certainly* and typed it and did not ask, and that was the correct professional behavior and the single most generous thing anybody in that building did for him all day.** Finally, the corridor outside the survey office at the works yard — eleven meters, a door at each end, a window that looks at a wall — at ten past three, with a folder under his right arm: **Nobody in that building is doing anything crooked and there is nothing for me to hold.** Then four more minutes standing there, because the office at the end of that corridor is where a determination will eventually be made about a district, and he is not going to be let into it, and nobody had done a single thing to stop him, **and the whole of it was a form that had been correct on the day it was written and had never had a field for the day it was written on.**

### Chapter 105 — Nineteen Cutbank Row *(batch midpoint)*

Wednesday, day eighty-eight, the third of December, from about eleven until dark. **Number nineteen on Cutbank Row is two rooms and a landing and a boiler that has to be lit before anything works, and the woman who lives in it says, from the step, before he has said anything: shut that behind you, the landing's cold, there's a draught comes down it that the landlord's been not fixing since before he died. The landlord died in 2038. She told him that before he had said anything, and he understood that she was not making conversation.** Cliff fifty-eight, Della eighty-eight, the man behind the wall eighty-eight; he got the 11 at a quarter to nine and had read the same page of the same folder on the bus. **Her address is on it. It says `PARTIAL` in the third column and `TRANSITIONAL` in the fourth. That was correct, and it was the thing he could not get past for the whole of that day: the two columns were right, and she was in a two-room flat, and the classification of a two-room flat is `PARTIAL`, and nobody had to decide anything for a seventy-one-year-old woman to be absent from a document about her own address.** Ezinne Abioye, seventy-one, at number nineteen since 1998; lets him in with the chain still on and takes the chain off when he has got his one hand out of his coat, which he thought was decent of her. *Sit down. Not that chair, that chair is for the table.* — *You will want to know why I am not angry. — Yes. — And I am not going to tell you, because if I tell you that then you will write it down and it will be the reason for a thing, and I am not a reason for a thing. I am going to tell you what I have worked out, and then you can go away and do what you are going to do.* **She worked it out herself, on her own, with no document, and she had worked it out before any of it.** They came with the rates, a card with a program on it and a number, and a line saying her address was not in the retained district, and she read it about four times and understood at the fourth because she has been a person at an address for fifty-five years. **They are not putting me anywhere. They are putting my address somewhere, and I am at the address.** She says it once and does not say it again for four hours. *Do you understand what I am telling you. — I do not think you do, and I think you have come here to be upset on my behalf and I have had that from three people since September and I have been very polite about it and I would rather not do it again.* And the whole of it, in her own mouth: **nobody has come and said you are going; there is nowhere to be going to; there is a wall going up at the end of the road in about eleven months; the water that comes up this street in a bad year is the river backing up and not the sea; I have seen the river back up in this street in 2031 and it is not the end of the world. They are taking a piece of ground to build a thing against water, and my two rooms are on the piece of ground, and my two rooms are `PARTIAL`, and `PARTIAL` is a word for a building, and it is correct, and it is the only word in that card that anybody wrote down by accident.** Then: *Nothing. I am not a household and I have not been a household since 1998 and there is nothing on any paper in this city that says I am one, and I would like that noticed, because I worked it out before September and I have been living on it for forty-four years.* **He has a folder in his hand and does not open it.** There is a moment in a thing like this where a man with a folder opens the folder; he came eleven minutes on a bus with a folder; the folder is the only thing in the room that is on his side, and it is the program's own document, and opening it would have been the whole of his purpose in the room. **He puts it on the chair behind him instead. He had been four seconds from opening it, noticed himself four seconds too late, and then sat there for the rest of the afternoon and was no use at all.** *What do you do, then. — I am a rescue service field technician. I am on suspension from one kind of work and I am not on suspension from the rest of it. I am in a building on Ash Street that was a refuge after a tunnel came in on the sixth of September, and I have three endorsements that were taken off me on the twenty-seventh of November and they are not coming back and nobody has to give them back.* — *And are you any good. — I was. — Are you now. — Not for this. That is what I have spent four days finding out.* — **That is better than saying you are going to fix it.** Then four hours, and he writes none of it and could not have reproduced more than a third: **the boiler, lit at seven every morning, two hundred and forty pounds in 2039, a man came in March and did not come back, it is going to go at some point and she has got as far as the thought and no further. The stairs — one flight to her own door, three flights to the street in bad weather. And Adaeze, thirty-four, on the floor above, who has been doing the stairs since the spring without being asked and without saying anything about it; Ezinne has tried three times to give her something and been refused three times, and the fourth time left a bag of oranges outside the door with no note on it, and Adaeze took the oranges and left a jar of something on the way past on the Tuesday, and neither of them has discussed it, and it has been going on for eight months. Her sister, died 2019, nine years at number nineteen, also `PARTIAL`. And a whole hour of it about the yard.** At about three: *You have not asked me one thing about the sea wall. Everybody asks me about the sea wall. There has been a man from the board in this kitchen and a woman from a newspaper in September, and all three of them asked me about the sea wall, and it is the biggest thing that has ever happened in this street and it is going to happen in about eleven months, and it is not what is going to happen to me.* — *What is going to happen to you.* — **The yard gets sun until about two. In December you get it about half eleven and that is the whole of it. And the boiler. And Adaeze on the stairs, and whether she keeps doing it when there is no boiler and no reason for it.** At half past three he asks the one question he has to ask, on the edge of the business: *Is there anybody in this street whose name is on anything.* She looks at him. *That is a strange question to ask a woman over her tea on a Wednesday. Most people ask it as a way of asking whether anybody in this street is all right. You are not doing that.* And the answer, exactly: **no — nobody in this street has their name on anything, I would know; there is a woman at number thirty-one who has been on this row forty-six years and every person on this street calls her by her husband's name, and the postal office calls her by her husband's name, and I have never once seen her be Mrs anything on any paper she has been handed in her life, and she is seventy-nine and she does not know there is anything to be. And I will tell you the true thing, which is that I have been aware of that since about 1998 and I have never done a thing about it, because it is her business and because I could not think of a single sentence that would not make it worse.** He writes it down **because she said it and not because it is useful, and it is not useful**, and the folder in the room is four hundred and twelve retained addresses and this street is not one of them, and the sentence would do nothing at all for anybody. *You are writing it down. — Yes. — Because I said it. — Because you said it. — All right,* and she pours the kettle again and never mentions it again. He stays until dark because there is a bus every twenty minutes and no reason to be there; **she does not offer him anything to eat and does not make an excuse, and he understands both of those.** She puts the chain back on and then takes it off again, which is a thing that means two opposite things. He starts *If there is anything you need —* and stops, **because he had been four seconds from it on Wednesday morning and had caught it then, and he said it anyway at six in the evening like a man who has been in a warm room, and it was the sentence, and she had told him three hours earlier that she had had it from three people since September.** *No. But you will come again, won't you, and you do not have to, and I am not asking you to, and I am telling you that you will.* — *Yes.* — **That is not a promise. I do not want a promise off a man with one hand and a folder. Come on Thursday if it rains and if it does not rain, and shut the door behind you on the landing.** He gets the 11 at ten past seven; the driver looks at him when he pays in coins and he puts two pound notes on the seat and says nothing, which was the correct arrangement on both sides. **The back hurt. He had been sitting on a wooden chair in a two-room flat on Cutbank Row for six hours, having been of no use to anybody at all, and he had paid eleven pounds in fares, and he had learned one thing that could not be put in a paragraph.**

### Chapter 106 — Margin, List, And Then Ash Street

Thursday, day eighty-nine, the fourth of December. Cliff fifty-nine, Della eighty-nine, the man behind the wall eighty-nine. **He gets the 11 at ten past eight with a folder under his right arm**, and before that, on the landing at number nineteen, a woman of seventy-one has read a program card out loud to him with the door shut behind them and has then turned it over and written a date on the back of it in pencil in front of him and put it in a drawer: *That is not the sheet. It is a card they gave me in September and I have kept it because I keep everything. **It is not a record. It is so that I know how old it is.*** The card: **19 CUTBANK ROW. STRUCTURE: TWO ROOMS. OCCUPANCY CLASS: PARTIAL. STATUS: TRANSITIONAL**, and in the bottom corner of the back of it, in soft pencil, in a hand that has been keeping things for eleven years, a date and nothing else: **18 SEP 42.** **He has spent twelve weeks behind a wall with nineteen hands on it, and six days in this district on the fact that a column exists, and is right, and is about a building — and three months have gone by in which nobody in this city has asked a woman why she dates her own paperwork in pencil.** Mara does a session at ten with six people in it, makes him hold a box of gloves and a bowl, does the work with both her hands and does not talk for twenty minutes, and then gives him one number and no meaning: **ninety-one people have come to the health center with a letter in eleven weeks, every one of those letters says the same four things in the same order; that is a count of people, and I am telling you the number and not what it means, because you will do what you want with a number and it is not my job to help you or to stop you.** At the works yard, the survey office has a trestle, a kettle, and a window that looks at a wall, and **Elias asks for the second reading at eleven and gets it in about nine seconds, which is the first thing that went wrong with his week, because he spent four days building it up.** Anum unrolls it without asking what it is for: **the twenty-ninth of November is the new one; the eleventh of September is the old one and it is in the tube.** The same soft line, same three colors, same west bank. The September roll goes over the top and the two lines are four inches apart along about two streets and on top of each other everywhere else. *What I have got is two pieces of paper about the same district and neither of them knows the other one exists.* — **Count it yourself. Take the overlay and the pen, and do not put the schedule on the same table.** He counts for fifty minutes with his right forearm flat on the overlay. **Forty-four addresses along two streets, and not a blob.** *Good. That is ours, and it went in on the twenty-ninth and it came out on the first and nobody rebuilt anything, and I would rather you had it in the right order than I gave it to you.* **Every single one of the forty-four says `TRANSITIONAL`. Not one says `RETAINED`.** He had six hundred and ninety-seven rows in front of him and forty-four streets' worth of new measurement, and the two of them did not disagree about anything, because a schedule cannot disagree with a reading. **And Anum answers the question he was always going to be asked, once, and says he is not to ask it a second time: if you built the schedule off the band every time the band moved, you would be rehoming four hundred people in an afternoon, and you would do it badly, because there is no version of an afternoon that is not bad, and people die in the moving; I have got a man in this yard whose entire job is not to have that happen twice, and this district has already had it happen, and I have not slept properly since the twenty-ninth of November and I am not going to be told by a man on a bus that I got it wrong on purpose.** Then a laptop, and forty minutes of silence, and he was not being cold. **Elias does not ask him a second time, and nobody in that room sees that either.** **Nerys Hobbs, forty-seven, hard hat under one arm, comes down the eleven-meter corridor at half past one at a speed that had nothing to do with him in it, and stops.** *Miss Hobbs. If I asked you about the works—* — **I am going to stop you there, in a corridor, and say one thing about the corridor. I do not discuss the works in a corridor. Ask me in a room and I will answer it in a room, and if the answer in the room is no, it will have been said where somebody who was not me can write it down.** She goes. **He goes and stands somewhere else with his back against a different wall, on purpose: it is the first time in thirteen weeks he has had somebody who might have helped and let her walk, and he is not going to write that down.** At half past four, in the yard, because the survey office door is locked, he says the error out loud to a man who is not on his side and did not ask to be: **I have been in this district six days looking for a hand in a document, and there is not one, and I want to say that out loud to somebody who does not care whether I say it.** — There is not one. There is one man who signs the schedule and he signs it in a room in the Meridian, and if you want to be unpleasant to him about it you will have to do it when the next reading comes, and I am not writing to tell you when. — **And the reading on the twenty-ninth is a fact. I understand that.** — It is a fact, and the schedule is a fact, and one of them is three months older than the other one, and I have never been able to explain to a room that both are true and that nobody has lied. It is the worst property of the work and I would not be doing it if the alternative was a hole in the ground with a district over it. *Go home. It is a Thursday.* **Then the footnote, on page two of nineteen, in italics, four lines long, read on a bus on Monday and not understood, and understood in four minutes in a hostel on Thursday afternoon.** The last line is a field on a document dated the second of December 2042. **It was not a lie and not a mistake, and there was no box on that page the twenty-ninth of November could have gone in.** He writes three things in the notebook on a page he decided weeks ago not to tear out and crosses out two, both attempts to say what the page was, and leaves one: **two pieces of paper about the same street, made three months apart, and neither of them can find out what the other one said; I have the right question and I do not have the sentence; somebody in this city writes sentences about forms for a living and it is not going to be me.** He reads it, does not underline it, closes the notebook. **Then Ash Street, twenty to seven, corridor light lit, a chair left in the corridor; Petar at the board; nine people in the front room; Joon Park writing a line about item fourteen on the twenty-two-line custody schedule with the date in the margin, which is a thing he does and nobody has asked him to stop.** Elias puts the two rolls on the table end to end, the folder beside them, and says nothing. **And Joon Park asks the batch's question: both of these are about Cutbank Row, one is three months old, one is two days old, one is called a schedule and one is called a reading, and I have worked in a building for twelve weeks where the answer is on the right piece of paper and not the other one, so somebody is going to tell me in one sentence what the difference is, and it is going in a notebook either way, so I would like it to be the right sentence.** Tomas Vale is in the chair by the wall with a cup of tea he brought with him, and has said nothing for two hours, and nobody in that room is going to ask him about Tidegate, and Elias has not asked him anything at all. **He is free to teach it and says so: not about the district — about the method; it is mine to give, and that is the condition I put on it, and a method is worth nothing if only one man can use it. I have taught it to a man in a records room with nobody in it.** And then, in about four sentences, in the order he would answer it for a new method officer, and then he stops: **force goes somewhere, that is the whole of the ground, and a reading tells you where it will want to go under a structure, and that is a measurement, and you can go back tomorrow and get the same answer or a different one, and either way you know which of the two you are holding. — And the schedule. — A schedule tells you who is a household.** Without any weight at all, which is what made it land. **It is not smaller than the reading and it is not wrong, and it is not the same quantity. Somebody has to take the answer to the first question and put it into the second one, and that is where people disappear — not in the reading, because the reading does not know anybody, and not in the row, because the row is correct. In the conversion. A measurement and a list are not the same quantity, and the man who does the conversion is not doing either of the two jobs.** Nobody says anything for about four seconds. A woman at the back says **that's just**, in the voice of somebody who has said it in meetings, and it is not a hostile voice, and nobody answers her, and that was correct. **It is not just anything. It is also obvious. And no sentence a room can produce will hold both of those at once, and a room of nine people at nine o'clock on a Thursday did not have one, and that was the shape of the whole thing happening two miles and eleven minutes away, and nobody in that room said it out loud either.** Joon Park writes it down as it was said, on the same sheet as the other twenty-two lines, in his own hand, in the margin where he puts dates — **it did not become a twenty-third item, because he does not hold the folder and he does not hold the rolls.**

### Chapter 107 — The Request

Friday, day ninety, the fifth of December. Cliff sixty, Della ninety, the man behind the wall ninety. **Seven days in Tidegate, a folder, two rolls, the beginnings of an understanding, none of it evidence — and the first thing in the building that made any sense is a man of twenty-four at a table with a form.** Joon Park brought a public records request form from the county library on the bus, with a pen, and a folded piece of paper with what he wanted on it in his own handwriting in case they would not let him keep the form, and got up at five. *Subject. Series. Span. And no year, and I want to explain the no year to somebody in about four minutes because it is the only interesting thing on this page.* **The counter is on the ground floor of a building with a stone step and a queue of two people; it is not the cell's counter and it is not the board's.** The form wants four things. **Who is holding it** — regional, and the regional office is not in Tidegate and is on the far side of the city, and the form has to be handed in at a counter and not posted, because a posted form does not get a date. **Which series** — one box, no room for two, and eleven words on the back saying that a request for more than one series is two requests. *Verification log. Not the clearance record, not the shift book. The verification log.* And the counterwoman, who is competent and correct and has said it a lot: **If you want to know what we decided and when, that is the log. If you want to know what happened on a site, that is the works, and they are not us, and I would not put that in this form because you will get the wrong answer and it will be a correct wrong answer and you will not be able to tell the difference for a month.** — That is the second time this week somebody has told me the correct answer was the wrong one. — I am not going to be able to tell which one you mean. — Neither can I. That is why we are here. **The span** — free text, first of September 2042 to the date on the form. **And the year box is not free text; it is a menu, and if you fill it in, it overrides the span, and Joon Park puts his pen on it and leaves it there.** *I need two years. The regional program goes back further than this district does. The clearance history for this alignment starts in 2039, and there is a model year in 2039 on a board at the gate in Tidegate, and everything I actually want is in December. If I put 2042 in that box they will search 2042 and they will send me 2042, and 2042 is the year where the answer is we did not do anything until December. And it will be true, and I will not be able to do one thing with it.* — *And if you leave it blank?* — They search the whole span, which is longer, and they hold it longer, because a long request is a request that has to be looked at by a person who is not the person who took it. **Four to six weeks from the date it goes in. It is not us. We do not set it and we cannot shorten it and I am not going to tell you it can be shortened, because it can be shortened by a person and that is a favor and favors do not come back in the same form.** And then the other half, because nobody says the other half of it: **four to six weeks is not the service refusing you, it is the service being able to tell you what it did. Anything that came back sooner than that and you are asking a person to remember, and a person who has to remember is a person who is guessing, and you do not want a guess in a file. If somebody in this building told you it could be done in ten days they have either broken a rule for you or told you a thing that is not true, and I would rather you knew that now than in January.** He signs it and does not put a year in. **NEAR END: 2 JANUARY 2043. FAR END: 16 JANUARY 2043**, written on the back of his own paper and then said out loud in the front room that evening, in the middle of a conversation about a light fitting, to about thirty people, and not put on the pad: *On Friday I filed a request for a document that is held in another building, and the service says four to six weeks, which means the second of January or the sixteenth. I cannot make it come sooner, I have not asked anybody to make it come sooner, and anybody who wants to know the date can have the date, and anybody who wants me to say the date is sooner is going to be told no by me in a room rather than in a corridor. I am not the records keeper of this building. Petar is not the records keeper of this building. I am a man of twenty-four who went to a counter on Friday with a form and a pen, and that is the entire authority I have.* **Petar says put it on the board. — No. — That is the board for. — That board has got three questions on it and a clerk's column and nineteen hands and there is no room, and we are all going to have to build another one, and it is not going to be me. And I am not going to stand up in this room every Friday and read out a date, because the day I do that this is a room that waits for dates. Ask me on a Tuesday. I will tell you any Tuesday.** **Nobody argued with him** — and that is the part Elias does not understand for a week: in thirteen weeks that building had never once been told a thing it could not check, and a man of twenty-four can stand in a Friday evening and say *here is a date six weeks away and I have no way to move it* and thirty people hear that as information and not as an excuse. **Then Tidegate, a church hall off Marden Row booked for the hour by a group with no name on the booking sheet, a kettle in the corner, about forty people, more than the twelve who came to the first one in November.** He has the folder and the two rolls in a cardboard tube, because a man of forty-one told him on a Monday that a man on a bus is the reason things get damaged. **He gives the date out first, before anything about the schedule — step four, the fourth time anybody on that team has used it and the first time on a promise instead of a plan — and then waits, because step four is not only the saying.** Then fifty minutes of the rest: the reading is right, nobody is lying, the band moved on the twenty-ninth of November, the schedule is dated the second of December and did not move, and **he could not explain why that was not a scandal, and he did not pretend to.** A man of about fifty in a works jacket: *Is six weeks worth it. Because I have got a house and I have got until the end of January, and there is a woman in this hall who has got a ground-floor flat and there is a notice on that flat. Six weeks is six weeks to me, and I want to know whether the man who came on the bus knows that.* **And Elias, in the middle of a hall of forty people: No. I do not know whether it is worth it. I know that the six weeks are real and I know that the thing I am going to do with them is this. If it was shorter I would be a man who had asked for something and got it quickly, and you would be a room full of people who had been told a thing six weeks early. I would rather be the second one for six weeks than the first one for six days, and I have not thought that out very well and I have thought about it a great deal.** *Right.* Then *he has got no answer* from behind him, and *he said so himself* from in front of him, and the row lasts about eleven seconds. **He walks back to the stop at half past nine and writes the two dates on the inside back cover of the notebook, because inside back covers are for things that will still matter in February, and understands that for four days he has been asking a district to be angry at a schedule, and that on Friday afternoon he handed the only thing he had got to a woman behind a stone counter and she put it in a queue, and the queue is six weeks long, and there is no part of that he is able to do on a Tuesday.** Two miles and eleven minutes, two pounds seventy-five each way, a back that hurt on the Tuesday.

### Chapter 108 — Saturday

Saturday, day ninety-one, the sixth of December, from about ten until dark. Cliff sixty-one, Della ninety-one, the man behind the wall ninety-one, inside a tunnel sealed for fifty-eight days and classified in nine words. **The Tidal Board and the cell's public counter shut at six on Friday night and are shut until Monday, and about a hundred and forty people in Tidegate have a Saturday, and there is nobody in the district whose job it is to tell them the truth about a schedule.** He gets the 11 at ten past eight and is not on his own time any more and has not been since Monday, and has told nobody and nobody has asked. **The corner of Cutbank Row and the yard approach, ten to half past ten, is the best thing in that district and it takes an hour and ten minutes to happen:** a woman in a fluorescent vest with a folding table out of a van and a stack of leaflets, saying one sentence to anybody who passes, and to him *most people do, love*; forty-one thousand of those went out with the rates, there is one on every door, and **I hand them out on a Saturday for the ones that go in the post and get put on a sideboard. Nobody looked at the table.** Then at about five past eleven, **Winifred Alagoa comes round the corner from the post office with the folded sheet in her right hand, stops at the bollard, turns the sheet so it faces the street, runs her finger down the left-hand column, and says the numbers out loud. She does not make an announcement.** *Rennard Street. One to thirty-one. Nineteen, and the one at the top has been empty since August, and I have a date for it because I went and looked.* Thirty-one people stop; nobody is asked to; a man with a dog listens to about four numbers and goes; two women come out of the post office; a man from the yard crosses the road to hear whether his own street is on it. *That's her. That's the one she does.* And then: **I do Rennard, and I do Murrow, and I do Marlow, and Sallow Court I do last, because I have to be careful with Sallow Court and I am not telling you why until I have got to the end of it.** It goes on for about an hour. **Elias stands on the far side of Cutbank Row for fifty-five minutes and does not say one word to anybody, and at about twelve he understands what he is looking at, and it is not a discovery, it is the floor coming out. He had spent twelve weeks learning a method — three questions on a wall, Finding 3 used correctly by the people it was written for, step four said out loud before anything else so a room knows it was not told, a form made to tell the truth by the people in it — and the whole of the job is to get people to do the ordinary thing in a room without him in it. And it has been happening on a corner in another district every Saturday morning for eleven years, and it did not need him, and it did not need a wall, and it did not need anybody to be a rescuer or a field technician or a man with one hand. It needed a woman with a sheet of paper and a bollard and five hours. Thirty-one people, and not one of them thought it was remarkable**, which is what undid him, because his whole education had been conducted in rooms where the fact that a thing was happening was visible from the street. **He has a leaflet in his pocket he has carried for seven days and shown to nobody, and it is the program of a regional pilot program with a Director's name at the bottom, and it is better paper than anything the Continuity Office put on a table in eleven weeks, and it is in every kitchen in this district, and it cost nothing and did more in a fortnight than his building did in twelve weeks.** **Then the yard, which runs six days, and the Saturday is when the program's own people are most visible. The canteen is doing lunch and the foreman wants a hand with a trestle. Nev Peart, fifty-one, foreman nineteen years, keeps a shift book in his own hand on the tail of the bench by the kettle with a rubber band round it, and it is not the terminal.** The trestle takes two men, and about fifteen meters from the canteen door to the gate, and the route is not straight because of the generator. **He carries it eleven times** — a fact about his grip and about being thirty-five and carrying things out of buildings since he was twenty-one; by the fifth he had stopped being careful, by the eighth he had the rhythm that works with a right forearm under a rail, and **by the eleventh a woman he has not met comes out of the canteen door with two mugs, stops, and looks at him.** *You're the one that got off the eleven on Wednesday. — With the folder. — Yes. — Right.* **That was the entire relationship the district had with him at the end of the day, and it was better than the one he came for.** Then, at half past three, the folder out on the trestle with the tube against his leg and the notice board behind him and nine people in front of him: **Before I say anything else I am going to tell you that I have not told this yard what I am about to say, and I am telling you now and not afterwards.** Step four, and it comes out of him without a decision, which is what a step is. *There is a document. It is nineteen pages. It is dated the second of December and it has six hundred and ninety-seven rows and two hundred and eighty-five of them say a word that means your address is not in the district that is being kept, and that word is on your card and you have all had it, and here is the number—* **And he stops, in the middle of it, with the folder open.** Because he has no version history and no forum and no standing in a district he has been in eight days; every one of those nine is going to believe him, and the only reason is that he is a stranger with a folder and a good coat, and by Monday it would be in every kitchen on that row; **there is not one person in Tidegate who has any obligation whatsoever to answer him, and if he is wrong the number will be in the district and he will be the only man in the country who can take it back. He had spent twelve weeks in a building learning that a document can be made to tell the truth. He was the one who had been wrong, in a corridor, on a Tuesday, and he had said it out loud to a wall.** He folds the folder shut: **I am going to stop. I have got no forum and nobody in this district has to answer me, and if I say that number in this yard you will believe me because I am a stranger and I have got a folder, and I have been wrong once this week already and I found out from a man who did not have to tell me.** Nine people look at him. Nobody says anything. Somebody at the back says *fair enough* in a voice that meant it; ten seconds; **and then the woman from the canteen says *Well, you'll be back on Monday then*, and goes back to the kettle.** **It was the first time in thirteen weeks he had stopped himself in a room, and there was no notice in any building in the city that told him he was allowed to.** The last hour is carrying and tea handed to him in a paper cup by a man who asks nothing. At about five he puts his hand on the shift book and **Nev Peart looks at him and does not open it, and Elias takes his hand away** — because on Wednesday a woman watched him write something down and was entitled to it and he had written it anyway, and on Thursday a man told him in a corridor that a thing said in a corridor is worth nothing, and on Friday a woman behind a stone counter told him that anything that came back sooner than four to six weeks is a person guessing. **Three people had told him something in three days that could not be used for anything, and all three had been right, and they had never met, and the only thing he had done about it was leave a rubber band where it was.** He gets the 11 at ten past six with his back very bad and a chemist's plaster on the second right knuckle that has come off and gone again inside his coat pocket, **and forty minutes of a Saturday had not been anybody's time but his own, and nobody had paid for it and nobody was going to.**

### Chapter 109 — Sallow Court

Sunday, day ninety-two, the seventh of December. Cliff sixty-two, Della ninety-two, the man behind the wall ninety-two. **Elias gets the 11 at ten past nine with Joon Park, who asked to come and was told there was nothing to come for and came.** The yard does not run on Sundays; the gate is shut; there are three works notices on the outside of it, and **he had walked past that gate on six days in one week and had not read one of them.**
> **WORKS NOTICE — 3 TO 27 SALLOW COURT · Clearance, 9 November 2042. Nights of 8 and 9. Residents relocated by arrangement. · Clearance complete, no outstanding.**
**Three lines and a date, with a newer notice about a skip and a newer one about a water supply underneath, and the paper on the first one the color paper goes to by the seventh of December. Nobody took it down, nobody covered it, nobody in that district had ever seen it, and it is not hidden, and no program officer has stood in front of it and said nothing, and it has been on a gate for twenty-eight days while a man with a folder went past with his eyes on a piece of paper in his hand.** *That is the whole of it. That is the entire record of a night and a night, and it is on a gate, and it is in the third person as far as anybody is concerned, which is what a word like complete is for.* Joon reads it twice. *Where is it kept.* — **I do not know. He does not know. Neither of us has asked, and neither of us knew it existed on Wednesday, and that is the part I do not like and the part I am not going to be able to put anywhere. — Which part is that. — That I have spent a week in a district reading documents out of a folder that somebody gave me on purpose, and did not walk to the end of the road.** Then Winifred Alagoa, door shut on the draught, boiler moving furniture in the next room, the sheet on the table where it lives. **I did Sallow Court last, and I did not tell you why, and I have thought about it since Saturday and I am going to tell you anyway, so you can decide what to do with it.** **Sallow Court is the one I get wrong. Not wrong wrong. I get it wrong and I get it right and the two things are the same number. In the spring I had thirty-nine doors on that block and I have thirty-nine now and the board's figure is forty-one, and I have known that since June and I have not done one thing about it, because I have a sheet and a sheet says thirty-nine and the sheet is what I can check with my feet.** — There is a difference of two. — **There is a difference of two and it has been sitting on my table since June and I have not told anybody about it because there is nobody to tell, and I am not going to start now and be a woman with a grievance at sixty-three.** Then the margin. **The margin of the sheet is about half an inch wide, on the inside of the second fold, where you would not see it if you were holding the sheet the way you hold a sheet, and there are four names written down the margin in a different pencil from the one the sheet is in.** Her account: **I did not have a pencil of my own on the night. I had a kitchen pencil. I wrote those four names on the ninth of November at about eleven at night with the kitchen pencil while a woman from the works stood in my doorway with a torch she did not need, and I did not know then that it was unusual that a woman should come with a torch to a street where there was no power. She was very polite. She asked me if I knew of anybody at three to twenty-seven who might be elderly and living alone, and I said there were four, and I gave her the four names, and she wrote them in a book she had, and then she went up the street. And about half an hour later I got the kitchen pencil out and I put them down on my own paper as well, because she had written them in a book and I did not know what the book was, and if the book went anywhere then the four went with it, and I am sixty-three and I have been keeping a sheet since 2031 because of a number on a fence and I do not need to be told twice.** And then, with the second pot on and not looking at him, the fact the chapter exists to deliver, in her mouth and once: **they are dead, and that is the part nobody has said to anybody; she knew by the Wednesday which end it had gone, nobody has come to this door, and she is not going to stand on a step in that street in December and ask a stranger about a woman she met once at a gate; their names are on neither the board's list nor her sheet, because neither one is a list of people, and the only place their names are written is that margin, and she would know, because she has been checking that board since 2031 and it has been wrong twice.** **Elias reads the four names twice and does not write them in the notebook** — not only restraint: the notebook is a thing he carries, the four were four people he had never met, he had no idea what he was entitled to write down, and a man in a hostel on a Sunday night could not be trusted with them because there was nobody who could tell him he had got one of them wrong. **And she offers them before he asks, and on her own terms: You are not having the sheet and I have said that to you and I will say it to the next one. You can have the four because the four are not on the sheet. The sheet is doors. Doors do not die. The four are in the margin in a kitchen pencil and they have been there since the ninth of November and they are the only copy in the world and I would like them to be in two places tonight, and I would like that to be my decision and not yours.** *It is my decision. I have been waiting eleven years for somebody to want something off this table and I am telling you what I am willing to have and I am not going to be thanked for it because I have not done anything for you.* **Then Joon Park asks the only question that matters, in four seconds, which is the fastest he does anything: Mr. Rook, I need to know what I am being handed. — What do you mean. — I mean is this a record. Because I hold things, and everything I hold is on a sheet with twenty-two lines on it, and every one of those twenty-two lines is a thing somebody asked me to hold and I said yes to, and the sheet is the reason I have never lost anything in twelve weeks. And if I put four names in my pocket on a Sunday morning then they are in a system, and in a month somebody is going to ask me where they came from, and I am going to have to say a woman's kitchen, and then they are in a system.** — **It is not a record. It is four names in a margin in a pencil in a kitchen and there is no second copy of anything.** — **Then I will take it anyway.** He writes the four names on the back of his own hand, in pencil, small, and does not put it on the sheet. **The taking is my decision and not hers, and I am not going to describe it as anything, and it does not go on here and it does not get a number, and if anybody in this city ever asks me where I got it I will say a woman on Cutbank Row wrote it down and that is the whole of what I will say.** *That is the correct answer, and it is the first correct answer anybody has given me about anything in about four years, and I would have said no to the word record. Do not call it a record in this room. It is a pencil.* — *It is a pencil.* And then the other half of it, which he has to be told and does not like any part of: **the works board's own address list for 3 to 27 Sallow Court says forty-one households, a printed schedule available to anybody who asks, out of the same envelope as the rates; Winifred Alagoa's sheet says thirty-nine doors for the same block, in her own hand, counted on foot, on a Saturday, for eleven years. Forty-one less thirty-nine is two.** And the four are in neither. *The four are in neither of them, and neither of those two things is a list of people, and that is why the four are in neither of them. Forty-one households is a number somebody wrote down about buildings. Thirty-nine doors is a number I wrote down about buildings. You could take both of those numbers to a meeting and have an argument about them for an hour and you would be arguing about two buildings. And a person cannot be inside the wrong number. I told a man that in 2031 and he wrote it down and went away.* *Two.* — **Two.
