half past eleven, with a row behind his name that says `evidence held, page not found` — a column blank since 1939 and used eleven times. **Sabina Hough is entered at half past twelve with her name, her address and the day, and Declan Hough asks whether putting his name against hers makes it true and is told no: it makes it a thing two people said, which is not nothing and is not a relationship being recorded.** **In the corridor outside number six, Joon Park has been on a chair for three hours with the pen cap on and says that every question asked them this morning was about a page or a column or a date, and not one of the three was asked what they want, and that he knows the difference between the two kinds of question and wants the second kind written down by somebody who is not him.** He goes home on the 11 at ten past three with a length of four-foot paper in the district that he has seen once from eight feet away and knows nothing about.

### Chapter 146 — She Reads It Herself

Tuesday, day one hundred and twenty-nine, the thirteenth of January 2043. Cliff ninety-nine, Della one hundred and twenty-nine, the man behind the wall one hundred and twenty-nine. **Hobbs turns the terminal off herself on Friday and reads the band with the works' own instruments** — a level, a tape, a steel pin driven into the verge at the low end of Rennard, four figures at 11:00, the same on Cutbank Row, the alignment walked end to end in an hour and twenty minutes, three days, every number written in her own hand on a works sheet with her own name against it, and she will not let a clerk type it. **She will not touch the reader and will not be in a room with the output, and what she asks the cell for is a flatbed, a driver and the fuel, in writing, as a vehicle and nothing else.** The vehicle comes at eight with no logo on the door and a driver told to keep the tip down and the wheels off the soft road, who says the same thing the low-loader's driver said on the eighth. **It agrees.** **The crew works the day block on 22–32 Rennard — the make safe, internal stair, first-floor access, the line that came out of a column headed NIGHT on the eighth and that she stopped at 11:26 — and it is five days late, and it is not a night and not a clearance and not on any sheet with a date on it; what comes off that street in daylight is what a crew of five could not get down it in the dark.** Five men and a ladder, and **Elias Rook carries things for nine hours** — a mattress, a chest of drawers, a television set, a board, a lamp, a bag of clothes, a folded pram, a jar of tools — with his right hand and his right shoulder and the flat of his forearm, along a landing a crew has cleared of doors and down to a flatbed, and he does not go up any stair anybody is on, does not go into any room, and learns not one name. **The thread is on under that street for the ninth hour and gives him nothing.** A man of about sixty-three at the top of a ladder says *you carried a door frame for two hours on the sixth* and *that was Marlow*, and goes back down. **At about five o'clock a second vehicle puts an offside wheel on the verge at about thirty miles an hour and she stops the works for eleven minutes and writes a number and the hour on a sheet.** **Peart writes *carried throughout* into Tuesday's page of the shift book against his own name and not Elias's — once in nineteen years, and Rook is not on that page because he is not the crew's — and says Hobbs stopped the works on a Tuesday and signed two lines and he stopped it on a Wednesday morning with a lorry on a soft road, and neither happened in a corridor and both are in writing.** **At about half past six on the Wednesday morning she signs the works' own day sheet for Tuesday — her own hand, the date, the hour, the eleven minutes, *stopped* against it — and the third of her four ruled lines stays blank, because it is not a line about a night that has not been walked. Two of four signed.** He gets the 11 at ten past seven, sits on a wall in the dark for an hour with his right arm against his chest, and works out that the whole of what the reading bought that Tuesday was bought by a woman with a tripod.

### Chapter 147 — I Have Been Reading A List As If It Were The Method

Wednesday, day one hundred and thirty, the fourteenth of January 2043. Cliff one hundred, Della one hundred and thirty, the man behind the wall one hundred and thirty. **Two cars from the Meridian at a quarter to ten.** The room above number six is booked from ten until one and **it is not a meeting** — one of them has asked for a room with a door and no table, one has asked to be told what the program is in words by somebody who is not paid by it, and the other has asked to come for an afternoon and be in the district and has not said why, and Wendy Tallow is not asking him about it because he is owed a room and a chair and not a question. **Sable Arden, fifty-eight, in the district for the first time, with no folder, and she opens by saying it is the smallest room she has ever given a briefing in and that the room is the correct size and she is aware of what that means.** Then, once, in her own words: **I authorized a method. I have been reading a list as if it were the method for four months because the list was working.** She does not soften it and will not be helped to, and there is no piece of paper about it anywhere. Asked whether she has read the schedule, she says she read it **on the second of December when it came out of the region**, again on Boxing Day, and this morning on a train with a pen, and that she put it in a drawer with the authorization and worked from it and never once compared it to anything on the ground, because nobody ever brought her anything from the ground, **and because the district she picked in September was the district where the ground was soft and the wall was cheapest, and that is not a coincidence, and she chose the ground she was given by a man in a shed and has been right about that for nine years.** **She is stopped and she is not forgiven: the conversion is hers, it is stopped by a one-line letter dated the fourteenth that she signed that morning and put with Selwyn, and the pilot authorization and the method are not withdrawn, and she says the schedule goes to a determination on Friday and she has not been asked whether she agrees and has agreed and has not been asked to be a hero about it.** The thread is off in that room and on eleven hundred yards away and gives him nothing. **Nathan Rusk, fifty-nine, alone, until twenty to one, says two things and no others: that a pilot is by definition a thing that can be stopped, and that stopping it is not a finding against anybody and the finding against anybody is in four years' time, and anybody waiting for somebody to say who was at fault will be standing on that corner in 2047 with the same sheet of paper.** Joon Park says he does not know what the finding is in four years, nobody does, and his sheet of twenty-two lines is not a form about the future, and Rusk says that is the correct use of a mouth.

### Chapter 148 — A Single Chosen Future Is Kinder

Thursday, day one hundred and thirty-one, the fifteenth of January 2043. Cliff one hundred and one, Della one hundred and thirty-one, the man behind the wall one hundred and thirty-one. **The church hall off Marden Row, about two hundred people, a working microphone, a kettle in the corner, and Marged Prosser starting it at seven minutes past seven and then sitting down beside the table.** **On a trestle by the door is four feet of unruled paper with four columns drawn in pencil, about a hundred and fifty names in about a hundred and fifty hands, and a fourth column that is empty on every line under a heading in capitals underlined twice that is a name.** Ferrand comes at five to seven and stands at the back for four minutes. **He says the word `Choir` once, like a man saying a union's name — *they call it the Bellwether Choir* — and says he will not explain it, and that if anybody in the Meridian wants to write to him about the word his answer is that he used it. He does not say it again, the program does not correct him, and nobody in that hall notices that the leaflet says BELLWETHER PILOT PROGRAM.** He asks the round himself, answers eleven, writes nothing down, and **says two hundred and eighty-five twice and does not apologize for it, and says the people who would like him to are mostly people who have not lost anybody this year.** Then, behind the table: **the duty flood officer in 2031, a school gym at ten past three in the morning, two reports, forty-one people, forty-one of them alive, and in the other report that school is under nine feet of water and there is no list anywhere of who was in it.** He puts the question to Elias — *you have never once been the man in the room when the water was coming and the reports disagreed* — and **Elias answers no, and that it is not because he is better at it, and that the answer takes eleven days and a shed and a woman with a sheet, and that he has not come to say it is enough.** **Marged Prosser puts the microphone in front of him and he does not fill the four minutes and Ferrand waits him out and does not rescue him.** Ferrand closes by refusing to guess at a determination that is not his instrument, saying two hundred and eighty-five again, and saying the two things he is worried about are that he might be wrong about the program and that they might be right, and that he cannot tell the difference from a platform. **About two hundred people believe him.** Inez Sato says nothing at all all evening.

### Chapter 149 — The Far End Is A Friday

Friday, day one hundred and thirty-two, the sixteenth of January 2043. Cliff one hundred and two, Della one hundred and thirty-two, the man behind the wall one hundred and thirty-two. **The last business day of the volume.** The envelope comes at eleven minutes past nine with three dates on it in three hands and the last one is today, and **nobody asks Joon Park anything, including Elias, and he is standing at the end of the counter with the schedule in his coat and does not look at it.** It is **a hundred and ninety-four pages** and it is a register of readings and instruments: occasion one on 11 September at 06:40, occasion two on 29 November at 06:15, **occasion three does not exist and there is a ruled line that says so**, occasion four on 1 January at 07:15 — and **in the margin against occasion two, in ink, four words: `Schedule raised 02.12 against occ. 1.`** **Anum says it is the whole of it, and refuses to put it in front of a board, because he wants a second occasion in writing that somebody signed and does not have it.** In the hearing room, which holds about thirty chairs and has forty-five people in it, **Nadia Kerr, 45, two years on the board, sits at the end of the front row with a folder she does not open — she asked the works in October for the nights it was going to work, was given four rows and no addresses, read it twice and did not ask again, and says nothing in the three hours and ten minutes.** **Rosalind Treloar, 61, chair, says unprompted and in her own words that she approved an alignment on a flood model adjusted twice and has never walked the whole of it and that there is no form in her building that says a commissioner has to stand on the ground.** **Aurelio Bexley, 54, puts the schedule and the margin side by side and says one sentence: this document records one occasion and that record has been applied to three others, and there is no field on it for the second, third or fourth and no field on it for a person.** **The board's surveyor, still unnamed, reads out four dates in ninety seconds and turns the notebook round on the table: Present. Confirmed.** The determination, read out at four minutes to two: **the securing band of 1 January is confirmed and remains in force; the Retention Schedule of 2 December 2042 is withdrawn; the page-two italic footnote is withdrawn with it, on the ground that a record of one occasion cannot be applied to another and that an office which prefers its record to its instrument preferred it once already, on 2 December, with forty-four addresses on the wrong side of it; the pilot authorization is not withdrawn, the reading continues, and nothing in it affects any other district, any other alignment, or any person's occupancy of any address.** **Nobody says a second number out loud and the man who drafted the footnote does not scratch anything out and lets the minute go by.** Nobody claps except about four people. On the steps, Anum says nobody in that room was cruel and it still cost four months and two hundred people a night in a hall. **Joon Park says he is not going to say the date a fourth time to anybody unless somebody asks him in a room, and today nobody asked, and the thing arrived.** Two people are on Cutbank Row with a length of paper and a pencil, and the fifth name on it is in a different pencil and is not the same as the other four.

### Chapter 150 — The Nail And The Back Of A School Exercise Book

Saturday, day one hundred and thirty-three, the seventeenth of January 2043. Cliff one hundred and three, Della one hundred and thirty-three, the man behind the wall one hundred and thirty-three. **The volume closes.** The depot on the far bank at ten past six, **on a day the depot does not run — there is no Saturday on any notice in that yard, the letter of the ninth said nothing about the days, and R. Dunleavy is in there with the gauge out and neither man says a word about it** — because the depot runs Tuesdays and Fridays at half past six and the Friday hose certification moved to Thursdays on the ninth of January by a letter that said the slots would not be held twice, gave nothing else and took nothing away, **and the Tuesday is the same Tuesday it has been since the end of October.** R. Dunleavy writes two figures in a book in his own hand and turns the book round and says he has got a hose and a gauge and a book. **The arithmetic is done out loud on a wall by the boatyard, to a man of about seventy painting a door who has not asked him anything:** twenty to five on a Tuesday against twenty to seven on every other morning is an hour and ten, fifty-two Tuesdays is sixty hours and forty minutes a year, which is two days and thirteen hours, which is one day for every calendar year and a half; the letter did not give him the Thursday and the Friday left is the inventory he has not held since the twenty-first of November, so the answer is two days and thirteen hours a year and he has got the arithmetic right twice and he is going to stop. **The run is over at twenty-four days and a hundred and thirty-two pounds in December and has not been restarted.** Then two people and a chair from the boatyard put **four feet of unruled paper on a nail above the counter at number two, because there is nowhere else in this district for it to go** — more than two hundred lines, about forty blank, 191 names in the fourth column counted by Alagoa on Friday night with a shopkeeper's pencil and already wrong by nine and about to be more wrong — and **the two of the four of the ninth of November that she released are on it, on the rows for eleven and nineteen Sallow Court, in her hand, in a different pencil from the other hundred and eighty-nine, and the other two are in half an inch of margin in a drawer upstairs, and the only other document in the city that ever held anything about those four doors is withdrawn and going into a file, and nobody is satisfied with any of it.** A woman of seventy-nine at thirty-one, who has been on that row forty-six years and is called by her husband's name by everybody including the post office, comes out at half past ten to look at the nail from four feet away and goes in. **The block being emptied tonight is fourteen to twenty-two Cutbank Row and thirty-one, and twenty-two is Alagoa's own flat: she has known since the sheets went round the shops in December, has said it to nobody, and there is no form in the city that asks a person whether they are inside one.** **The third night has been walked with four men and a ladder; three of four lines are signed and one is blank; the booking runs to Thursday the fifth of February; a foreman has told a yard he is going to hand the shift book in in February; the eleven cards in a drawer have a street on the back of each in his own hand, the first time in nineteen years a works card has carried one, and nobody wrote a form that asked for it.** The wall on Ash Street has three questions on it and no room, the bracket is empty, the nineteenth of November is still the only date, the four minutes and a lamp are in Petar's book, and nobody has taken anything off that wall and nobody is going to. **And in a two-room flat on Cutbank Row, past the sums, in a school exercise book bought for two pounds in a shop on Marden Row out of her own money, in a ballpoint, on a page with a sum on the other side: WHO IS INSIDE A LINE THAT WAS DRAWN ON A DIFFERENT DAY? — AND WHO SIGNS THE SCHEDULE? — written by a woman of sixty-three who says she is not writing it for a record, and has not been asked for it by anybody, and cannot sleep, and there is nowhere else to put it, and it is not going on the wall at yours and it is not going on the nail at hers.** He does not ask her for the book. She tells him to sit down, because he looks as though somebody has taken something off him.
